A Unifying Categorical View of Nondeterministic Iteration and Tests
Sergey Goncharov, Tarmo Uustalu

Abstract
We study Kleene iteration in the categorical context. A celebrated completeness result by Kozen introduced Kleene algebra (with tests) as a ubiquitous tool for lightweight reasoning about program equivalence, and yet, numerous variants of it came along afterwards to answer the demand for more refined flavors of semantics, such as stateful, concurrent, exceptional, hybrid, branching time, etc. We detach Kleene iteration from Kleene algebra and analyze it from the categorical perspective. The notion, we arrive at is that of Kleene-iteration category (with coproducts and tests), which we show to be general and robust in the sense of compatibility with programming language features, such as exceptions, store, concurrent behaviour, etc. We attest the proposed notion w.r.t. various yardsticks, most importantly, by characterizing the free model as a certain category of (nondeterministic) rational trees .

1
Introduction

Axiomatizing notions of iteration both algebraically and categorically is a
well-established topic in computer science where two schools of thought can be
distinguished rather crisply: the first one is based on the inherently nondeterministic

Kleene iteration
, stemming from the seminal work of Stephen Kleene
[
24
]
and deeply rooted
in automata and formal language theory; the second one stems from another seminal
work – by Calvin Elgot
[
12
]
– and is based on another notion of iteration,
we now call
Elgot iteration
. The most well-known instance of Kleene iteration is the
one that is accommodated in the algebra of regular expressions where
a^{\ast}
represents
n
-fold
compositions
a\cdots a
and
n
nondeterministically ranges over all naturals.
More abstractly, Kleene iteration is an operation of the following type:

\displaystyle\textbf{}~~\frac{p\colon X\to X}{p^{\ast}\colon X\to X}

Intuitively, we think of
p
as a program whose inputs and outputs range over
X
,
and of
p^{\ast}
as a result of composing
p
nondeterministically many times with itself.
Elgot iteration, in contrast, is agnostic to nondeterminism,
but crucially relies on the categorical notion of binary coproduct, and thus can only be implemented
in categorical or type-theoretic setting. Concretely, the typing rule for Elgot iteration
is

\displaystyle\textbf{}~~\frac{p\colon X\to Y+X}{p^{\dagger}\colon X\to Y}

(
\dagger
)

That is, given a program that receives an input from
X
, and can output either to

Y
or to
X
,
p^{\dagger}
self-composes
p
precisely as long as
p

outputs to
X
.

A profound exploration of both versions
of iteration and their axiomatizations in the categorical context, more precisely, in the
context of
Lavwere theories
, has been done by Bloom and Ésik in a series
of papers and subsumed in their monograph
[
6
]
. One outcome of this
work is that in the context of Lawvere theories, in presence of nondeterminism,
Kleene iteration and Elgot iteration are essentially equivalent – the ensuing
theory was dubbed
iteration grove theory

[
5
]
.
The existing analysis still does not cover certain aspects,
which we expressly address in our present work, most importantly, the following.

•

Lavwere theories are only very special categories, while iteration is a common
ingredient of semantic frameworks, which often involve it directly via an ambient category with coproducts,
and not via the associated Lavwere theory.

•

Previous results on the equivalence of Elgot iteration and Kleene iteration do not address
the connection between control mechanisms involved in both paradigms:
Elgot iteration fully relies on coproducts for making decisions whether to continue
or to end the loop, while Kleene iteration for the same purpose uses an additional mechanism of
tests

[
26
]
,
which are specified axiomatically and thus yield a higher degree of flexibility.

•

A key feature of Kleene iteration of Kleene algebra,
are the quasi-equational laws, which can be recast
[
16
]

to a form of the versatile and powerful
uniformity principle
(e.g.
[
39
]
).
The latter is parameterized by a class of well-behaved elements, which in Kleene
algebra coincide with the algebra’s entire carrier. However, in many situations, this class
has to be restricted, which calls for axiomatizing it, analogously to tests.

Here, we seek a fundamental, general and robust categorical notion of
Kleene iteration, which addresses these issues, is in accord with Elgot iteration
and the corresponding established laws for it (Elgot iteration operators that satisfy
these laws are called
Conway operators
). In doing so, we depart from the
laws of Kleene algebra, and relax them significantly. Answering the question how
to do this precisely and in a principled way is the main insight of our work.

Let us dwell briefly on the closely related issues of generality and robustness.
The laws of Kleene algebra, as originally axiomatized by Kozen
[
25
]
, capture a very
concrete style of semantics, mirrored in the corresponding free model, which is
the algebra of regular events, i.e. the algebra of regular sets of strings over
a finite alphabet of symbols, with iteration rendered as a least fixpoint.
Equations validated by this model are thus shared by the whole class of Kleene
algebras. By regarding Kleene algebra terms as programs, the interpretation over
the free model can be viewed as
finite trace semantics
of linear-time nondeterminism.
A standard example of properly more fine-grained – branching-time – nondeterminism is (bisimulation-based) process algebra,
which fails the Kleene algebra’s law of distributivity from the left:

\displaystyle p\mspace{};\mspace{.5}(q+r)=

\displaystyle\;p\mspace{};\mspace{.5}q+p\mspace{};\mspace{.5}r.

(1)

Similarly, if we wanted to allow our programs to raise exceptions, the laws of Kleene
algebra would undesirably force all exceptions to be equal:

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathsf{raise}}e_{1}=\operatorname{\mathsf{raise}}e_{1}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}0=0=\operatorname{\mathsf{raise}}e_{2}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}0=\operatorname{\mathsf{raise}}e_{2}.

Here, we combine the law
p\mspace{};\mspace{.5}0=0
of Kleene algebra with the equation

\operatorname{\mathsf{raise}}_{i}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}p=\operatorname{\mathsf{raise}}_{i}
that alludes to the common
programming knowledge that raising an exception exits the program instantly
and discards any subsequent fragment
p
. The resulting equality
\operatorname{\mathsf{raise}}e_{1}=\operatorname{\mathsf{raise}}e_{2}

states that raising exception
e_{1}
is indistinguishable from raising exception
e_{2}
.

We can interpret these and similar examples as evidence that the axioms of Kleene
algebra are not sufficiently robust under extensions by programming language features.
More precisely, Kleene algebras can be scaled up to
Kleene monads

[
18
]
,
and thus reconciled with Moggi’s approach to computational effects
[
33
]
. An important
ingredient of this approach are
monad transformers
, which allow for combining
effects in a principled way. For example, one uses the
exception monad transformer

to canonically add exception raising to a given monad. The above indicates that
Kleene monads are not robust under this transformer.

Finally, even if we accept all iteration-free implications of Kleene algebra, these will not
jointly entail the following identity:

\displaystyle 1^{\ast}=1,

(2)

which is however entailed by the Kleene algebra axioms. One setting where (
2
)
is undesirable is domain theory, which insists on distinguishing
deadlock

from
divergence
, in particular, (
2
) is failed by interpreting
programs over the
Plotkin powerdomain

[
36
]
. Intuitively,
(
2
) states that, if a loop
may
be exited, it
will
eventually be
exited, while failure of (
2
) would mean that the left program
may
diverge, while the
right program
must
converge, and this need not be the same. Let us call the corresponding
variant of Kleene algebra, failing (
2
),
may-diverge Kleene algebra
. However, it is not
a priori clear how the axioms of may-diverge Kleene algebras must look like, given that
(
2
) is not a Kleene algebra axiom, but a consequence of the
assumption that Kleene iteration is a least fixpoint. Hence, in may-diverge
Kleene algebras Kleene iteration is not a least fixpoint (w.r.t. the order, induced by
+
).

The notion we develop and present here is that of
Kleene-iteration category (with tests) (KiC(T))
.
It is designed to address the above issues and to provide a uniform general
and robust framework for Kleene iteration in a category. We argue in various ways
that KiC(T) is in a certain sense the most basic practical notion of Kleene iteration,
most importantly by characterizing its free model, as a certain category of (nondeterministic)
rational trees.

Related work

The (finite or
\omega
-complete) partially additive categories (PACs) by
Arbib and Manes
[
2
]
and the PACs with effects of Cho
[
8
]
are
similar in spirit to KiCs in that they combine structured homsets and
coproducts, but significantly more special; in particular they support relational,
sets of traces and similar semantics, but not branching time semantics.
A PAC is a category with coproducts
enriched in partial commutative monoids (PMC). The PMC structure of
homsets and the coproducts are connected by axioms that make the PMC
structure unique. In an
\omega
-complete PAC, these axioms also
ensure the presence of an Elgot iteration operator, which is computed as a least fixpoint. A PAC with effects
comes with a designated effect algebra object; this defines a wide
subcategory of total morphisms, with coproducts inherited from the
whole category. Effectuses
[
23
]
achieve the same as PACs
with effects, but
starting with a category of total morphisms and then adding partial
morphisms. Cockett
[
9
]
recently proposed a notion of iteration in a category, based on restriction
categories, and analogous to Elgot iteration (

†

‣
1
), but avoiding
binary coproducts in favor of a suitably axiomatized notion of disjointness for morphisms.

In the strand of Kleene algebra, various proposals were made with utilitarian motivations
to weaken or modify the Kleene algebra laws, and thus to cope with process algebra
[
14
]
, branching
behaviour
[
34
]
, probability
[
30
]
, statefulness
[
22
]
,
graded semantics
[
15
]
, without however aiming to identify the conceptual
core of Kleene iteration, which is our objective here. A recent move within this
tendency is to eliminate nondeterminism altogether, with
guarded
Kleene algebras

[
40
]
, which replace nondeterministic choice and
iteration with conditionals and while-loops. This is somewhat related to our analysis of
tests and iteration via while-loops, but largely orthogonal to our main objective
to stick to Kleene iteration as nondeterministic operator in the original sense.
Our aim to reconcile Kleene algebra, (co)products and Elgot iteration is rather
close to that of Kozen and Mamouras
[
27
]
.

Our characterization of the free KiCT in a way reframes the original Kozen’s characterization
of the free Kleene algebra
[
25
]
. We are not generalizing this result though, essentially because we
work in categories with coproducts, while a true generalization would only be achieved
via categories without any extra structure (noting that algebras are single-object categories). This distinction becomes particularly important
in the context of branching time semantics, which we also cover by allowing a controlled use of
programs that fail distributivity from the left (
1
). An axiomatization
for such semantics has been proposed by Milner
[
32
]
and was shown to be
complete only recently
[
21
]
. Again, we are not generalizing this
result, since the definability issues, known to be the main obstruction for
completeness arguments there, are not effective in presence of coproducts.

Plan of the paper

We review minimal notations and
conventions from category theory in
Section 2
. We then introduce
idempotent grove and Kleene-Kozen categories in
Section 3
to start off.
In
Section 4
, we formally compare two control mechanisms in categories:
decisions and tests. In
Section 5
,
6
, we establish
equivalent presentations of nondeterministic iteration as Kleene iteration, as
Elgot iteration and as while-iteration. In
Section 7
we construct a free
model for our notion of iteration, and then come to conclusions in
Section 8
.

2
Notations and Conventions

We assume familiarity with the basics of category
theory
[
29
,
3
]
. In a category
\mathbf{C}
,
|\mathbf{C}|
will denote the
class of objects and
\mathbf{C}(X,Y)
will denote the set of morphisms from
X
to
Y
. The judgement

f\colon X\to Y
will be regarded as an equivalent to
f\in\mathbf{C}(X,Y)
if
\mathbf{C}
is clear
from the context.
We tend to
omit indexes at natural transformations for readability. A subcategory
\mathbf{D}
of

\mathbf{C}
is called
wide
if
|\mathbf{C}|=|\mathbf{D}|
. We will use diagrammatic composition

\mspace{};\mspace{.5}
of morphisms throughout, i.e. given
f\colon X\to Y
and
g\colon Y\to Z
,
f\mspace{};\mspace{.5}g\colon X\to Z
.
We will denote by
1_{X}
, or simply
1
the identity morphism on
X
.

Coproducts

In this paper, by calling
\mathbf{C}
“a category with coproducts” we will always mean that

\mathbf{C}
has
selected binary coproducts
, i.e. that a bi-functor
\oplus\colon\mathbf{C}\times\mathbf{C}\to\mathbf{C}
exists
such that
X\oplus Y
is a coproduct of
X
and
Y
.
In such a category, we write

\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{0}}}\colon X\to X\oplus Y
and
\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{1}}}\colon Y\to X\oplus Y

for the left and right coproduct injections correspondingly. We will occasionally
condense
\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{i}}}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{j}}}
to
\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{i\,j}}}
for the sake of succinctness.

Monads

A monad
{\bm{\mathsf{T}}}
on
\mathbf{C}
is determined by a
Kleisli triple

(T,\eta,(-)^{\sharp})
,
consisting of a map
T\colon{|\mathbf{C}|\to|\mathbf{C}|}
, a family of morphisms

(\eta_{X}\colon X\to TX)_{X\in|\mathbf{C}|}
and
Kleisli lifting
sending each
f\colon X\to TY
to
f^{\sharp}\colon TX\to TY
and
obeying
monad laws
:

\displaystyle\eta^{\sharp}=1,

\displaystyle\eta\mspace{};\mspace{.5}f^{\sharp}=f,

\displaystyle(g\mspace{};\mspace{.5}f^{\sharp})^{\sharp}=g^{\sharp}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}f^{\sharp}.

It follows that
T
extends to a functor,
\eta
extends to a natural transformation –
unit
,

{\mu=1^{\sharp}}\colon TTX\to TX
extends
to a natural transformation –
multiplication
, and that
(T,\eta,\mu)

is a monad in the standard sense
[
29
]
. We will generally use blackboard capitals (such as
{\bm{\mathsf{T}}}
) to refer to monads
and the corresponding Roman letters (such as
T
) to refer to their functor parts.
Morphisms of the form
f\colon X\to TY
are called
Kleisli morphisms
and form the
Kleisli
category

\mathbf{C}_{{\bm{\mathsf{T}}}}
of
{\bm{\mathsf{T}}}
under
Kleisli composition

f,g\mapsto f\mspace{};\mspace{.5}g^{\sharp}

with identity
\eta
. If
\mathbf{C}
has binary coproducts then so does
\mathbf{C}_{\bm{\mathsf{T}}}
: the
coproduct injections are Kleisli morphisms of the form
\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{0}}}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}\eta\colon X\to T(X\oplus Y)
,

\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{1}}}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}\eta\colon Y\to T(X\oplus Y)
.

Coalgebras

Given an endofunctor
F\colon\mathbf{C}\to\mathbf{C}
, a pair
(X\in|\mathbf{C}|,c\colon X\to FX)

is called an
F
-coalgebra. Coalgebras form a category under the following notion
of morphism:
h\colon X\to X^{\prime}
is a morphism from
(X,c)
to
(X^{\prime},c^{\prime})
if
h\mspace{};\mspace{.5}c^{\prime}=c\mspace{};\mspace{.5}Fh
.
A terminal object in this category is called a
final coalgebra
. We reserve
the notation
(\nu F,\mathsf{out})
for a selected final coalgebra if it exists. A well-known
fact (Lambek’s lemma) is that
\mathsf{out}
is an isomorphism.

For a coalgebra
(X,c\colon X\to FX)
on
\mathbf{Set}
a relation
\mathcal{B}\subseteq X\times X
is
a
(coalgebraic) bisimulation
if it extends to a coalgebra
(\mathcal{B},b\colon\mathcal{B}\to F\mathcal{B})
, such
that the left and the right projections from
\mathcal{B}
to
X
are coalgebra morphisms;

x\in X
and
y\in X
are
bisimilar
if
x\mathrel{\mathcal{B}}y
for some bisimulation
\mathcal{B}
;
the coalgebra
(X,c\colon X\to FX)
is
strongly extensional

[
41
]

if bisimilarity entails equality. Final coalgebras are the primary example of
strongly extensional coalgebras.

3
Idempotent Grove and Kleene-Kozen Categories

A monoid is precisely a single-object category. Various algebraic structures
extending monoids can be generalized to categories along this basic observation
(e.g. a group is a single-object groupoid, a quantale is a single-object quantaloid, etc.).
In this section, we consider two classes of categories for nondeterminism and Kleene
iteration,
which demonstrate our principled
categorical approach of working with algebraic structures.

Definition 1

(Idempotent Grove Category, cf.
[
4
,
5
]
)
.

Let us call a category
\mathbf{C}
an
idempotent grove category

if the hom-sets of
\mathbf{C}(X,Y)
are equipped with the structure
(0,+)
of bounded
join-semilattice such that,
for all
p\in\mathbf{C}(Y,Z)
and
q,r\in\mathbf{C}(X,Y)
,

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad 0\mspace{};\mspace{.5}p=

\displaystyle\;0,

\displaystyle(q+r)\mspace{};\mspace{.5}p=

\displaystyle\;q\mspace{};\mspace{.5}p+r\mspace{};\mspace{.5}p.

(3)

In such a category, we call a morphism
p\in\mathbf{C}(X,Y)

linear
if it satisfies, for all
q,r\in\mathbf{C}(Y,Z)
,

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad p\mspace{};\mspace{.5}0=

\displaystyle\;0,

\displaystyle p\mspace{};\mspace{.5}(q+r)=

\displaystyle\;p\mspace{};\mspace{.5}q+p\mspace{};\mspace{.5}r.

(4)

An
idempotent grove category with coproducts
is an idempotent grove
category with selected binary coproducts and with
\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{0}}}
and
\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{1}}}
linear.

Given
p,q\in\mathbf{C}(X,Y)
, let
p\leq q
if
p+q=q
. This
yields a partial order with
0
as the bottom element, and morphism composition
is monotone on the left, while linear morphisms are additionally monotone on the right.
The class of all linear morphisms of an idempotent grove category thus forms
a sub-category enriched in bounded join-semilattices (equivalently: commutative
and idempotent monoids) – thus, an idempotent grove category where all morphisms are
linear is an enriched category. However, we
are interested in categories where not all morphisms are linear. An instructive example
is as follows.

Example 2

(Synchronization Trees)
.

Let
A
be some non-empty fixed set of
labels
, and let
TX=\nu\gamma.\,{\mathcal{P}}_{\omega_{1}}(X\oplus A\times\gamma)

where
{\mathcal{P}}_{\omega_{1}}
is the countable powerset functor. By generalities
[
42
]
,
T

extends to a monad
{\bm{\mathsf{T}}}
on
\mathbf{Set}
. The elements of
TX
can be characterized
as
countably-branching strongly extensional synchronization trees with exit
labels in
X
. Synchronization trees have originally been introduced by Milner
[
31
]

as denotations of process algebra terms, and subsequently generalized to
infinite branching and to explicit exit labels (e.g.
[
1
]
).
A generic element
t\in TX
can be more explicitly represented using the following
syntax:

\displaystyle t=\sum_{i\in I}a_{i}.\,t_{i}+\sum_{i\in J}x_{i}

where
I
and
J
are at most countable, the
a_{i}
range over
A
, the

t_{i}
range over
TX
, and
x_{i}
range over
X
. The involved summation operators

\sum_{i\in I}
are considered modulo countable versions of associativity,
commutativity and idempotence, and
0=\sum_{i\in\emptyset}t_{i}
and
t_{1}+t_{2}=\sum_{i\in\{1,2\}}t_{i}
.

Recall that strong extensionality means that bisimilar elements are equal
[
41
]
.
The Kleisli category of
{{\bm{\mathsf{T}}}}
is idempotent grove with
0
and
+
inherited from
{\mathcal{P}}_{\omega_{1}}
and
ensuring (
3
) automatically.
It is easy to see that linear morphisms are precisely those that do not
involve actions.

A straightforward way to add a Kleene iteration operator to a category
is as follows.

Definition 3

(Kleene-Kozen Category
[
16
]
)
.

An idempotent grove category
\mathbf{C}
is a
Kleene-Kozen category
if all morphisms of
\mathbf{C}

are linear and there is a
Kleene iteration
operator

(\operatorname{-\!-})^{\ast}\colon\mathbf{C}(X,X)\to\mathbf{C}(X,X)

such that, for any
p\colon X\to X
,
q\colon Y\to X

and
{r\colon X\to Z}
, the morphism
q\mspace{};\mspace{.5}p^{\ast}
is the least (pre-)fixpoint of
q+(\operatorname{-\!-})\mspace{};\mspace{.5}p

and the morphism
p^{\ast}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}r
is the least (pre-)fixpoint of
r+p\mspace{};\mspace{.5}(\operatorname{-\!-})
.

It is known
[
16
]
that Kleene algebra is precisely a single-object
Kleene-Kozen category.

In idempotent grove categories with coproducts, the following property is a direct consequence
of linearity of
\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{0}}}
,
\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{1}}}
, and will be used extensively throughout.

Proposition 4
.

In idempotent grove categories with coproducts,
[p,q]+[p^{\prime},q^{\prime}]={[p+p^{\prime},q+q^{\prime}]}
.

4
Decisions and Tests in Category

We proceed to compare two mechanisms for modeling control in categories:

decisions
and
tests
. The first one is inherently categorical, and requires
coproducts. The second one needs no coproducts, but requires nondeterminism. The
latter one is directly inspired by tests of the Kleene algebra with tests
[
26
]
.
We will show that tests and decisions are in a suitable sense equivalent, when it comes
to modeling control that satisfies Boolean algebra laws.

Definition 5

(Decisions
[
10
,
16
]
)
.

In a category
\mathbf{C}
with binary coproducts, we call morphisms from
\mathbf{C}(X,X\oplus X)

decisions
.

We consider the following operations on decisions, modeling truth values
and logical connectives:
\operatorname{\mathsf{t\kern-0.45ptt}}=\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{1}}}
(true),
\operatorname{\mathsf{f\kern-0.78ptf}}=\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{0}}}
(false),
\mathrel{\texttt{{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\,\texttildelow}}}}\kern-1.5ptd=d\mspace{};\mspace{.5}[\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{1}}},\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{0}}}]
(negation),

d\mathrel{\texttt{|\kern-2.95pt|}}e=d\mspace{};\mspace{.5}[e,\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{1}}}]
(disjunction),
d\mathrel{\texttt{\&\kern-0.5pt\&}}e=d\mspace{};\mspace{.5}[\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{0}}},e]
(conjunction).
Even without constraining decisions in any way, certain logical properties
can be established, e.g. (not necessarily commutative or idempotent) monoidal
structures
(\operatorname{\mathsf{f\kern-0.78ptf}},\mathrel{\texttt{|\kern-2.95pt|}})
,
(\operatorname{\mathsf{t\kern-0.45ptt}},\mathrel{\texttt{\&\kern-0.5pt\&}})
, involutivity of
\mathrel{\texttt{{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\,\texttildelow}}}}\kern-1.5pt
, “de Morgan laws”

\mathrel{\texttt{{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\,\texttildelow}}}}\kern-1.5pt(d\mathrel{\texttt{|\kern-2.95pt|}}e)=\mathrel{\texttt{{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\,\texttildelow}}}}\kern-1.5ptd\mathrel{\texttt{\&\kern-0.5pt\&}}\mathrel{\texttt{{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\,\texttildelow}}}}\kern-1.5pte
,
\mathrel{\texttt{{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\,\texttildelow}}}}\kern-1.5pt(d\mathrel{\texttt{\&\kern-0.5pt\&}}e)=\mathrel{\texttt{{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\,\texttildelow}}}}\kern-1.5ptd\mathrel{\texttt{|\kern-2.95pt|}}\mathrel{\texttt{{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\,\texttildelow}}}}\kern-1.5pte
,
and the laws
\operatorname{\mathsf{t\kern-0.45ptt}}\mathrel{\texttt{|\kern-2.95pt|}}d=\operatorname{\mathsf{t\kern-0.45ptt}}
,
\operatorname{\mathsf{f\kern-0.78ptf}}\mathrel{\texttt{\&\kern-0.5pt\&}}d=\operatorname{\mathsf{f\kern-0.78ptf}}
.

Given
d\in\mathbf{C}(X,X\oplus X)
and
p,q\in\mathbf{C}(X,Y)
, let

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathsf{\underline{if}}}\mathbin{}d\operatorname{\mathsf{\underline{then}}}\mathbin{}p\operatorname{\mathsf{\underline{else}}}\mathbin{\,}q=d\mspace{};\mspace{.5}[q,p].

(5)

Definition 6

(Tests)
.

Given an idempotent grove category
\mathbf{C}
, we call a family of linear morphisms

\mathbf{C}^{?}={(\mathbf{C}^{?}(X)\subseteq\mathbf{C}(X,X))_{X\in|\mathbf{C}|}}

tests
if every
\mathbf{C}^{?}(X)

forms a Boolean algebra under
\mspace{};\mspace{.5}
as conjunction and
+
as disjunction.

It follows that
1\in\mathbf{C}^{?}(X)
and
0\in\mathbf{C}^{?}(X)
correspondingly are the top and bottom elements
of
\mathbf{C}^{?}(X)
. Given
b\in\mathbf{C}^{?}(X)
,
p,q\in\mathbf{C}(X,Y)
, let

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathsf{if}}\mathbin{}b\operatorname{\mathsf{then}}\mathbin{}p\operatorname{\mathsf{else}}\mathbin{}q=b\mspace{};\mspace{.5}p+\bar{b}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}q.

(6)

In an idempotent grove category
\mathbf{C}
with coproducts and tests
\mathbf{C}^{?}
, let
?\colon\mathbf{C}(X,X\oplus X)\to\mathbf{C}^{?}(X)
be the morphism

d?=d\mspace{};\mspace{.5}[0,1].

Proposition 7
.

Let
\mathbf{C}
be an idempotent grove category with coproducts. If a decision
d
is linear, then, for all
p
and
q
, we have

\operatorname{\mathsf{\underline{if}}}\mathbin{}d\operatorname{\mathsf{\underline{then}}}\mathbin{}p\operatorname{\mathsf{\underline{else}}}\mathbin{\,}q=\operatorname{\mathsf{if}}\mathbin{}d?\operatorname{\mathsf{then}}\mathbin{}p\operatorname{\mathsf{else}}\mathbin{}q
.

Let us say that a pair
(b,c)\in\mathbf{C}(X,X)\times\mathbf{C}(X,X)
satisfies
(the law of)
contradiction
if
b\mspace{};\mspace{.5}c=0
, and that it satisfies
(the law of) excluded middle

if
b+c=1
.
The following characterization is instructive.

Proposition 8
.

Given an idempotent grove category
\mathbf{C}
, a family of linear morphisms
\mathbf{C}^{?}={(\mathbf{C}^{?}(X)\subseteq\mathbf{C}(X,X))_{X\in|\mathbf{C}|}}

forms tests for
\mathbf{C}
iff, for every
b\in\mathbf{C}^{?}
, there is
\bar{b}\in\mathbf{C}^{?}
such that

(b,\bar{b})
satisfies contradiction and excluded middle.

Note that the smallest choice of tests in
\mathbf{C}
is
\mathbf{C}^{?}(X)=\{0,1\}
. We
proceed to characterize the smallest possible choice of tests, sufficient for modeling
control.

Definition 9

(Expressive Tests)
.

We call the tests
\mathbf{C}^{?}

expressive
if every
\mathbf{C}^{?}(X)
contains
[\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{0}}},0]

whenever
X=X_{1}\oplus X_{2}
.

Lemma 10
.

The smallest expressive family of tests always exists and is obtained by closing
tests of the form
0
,
1
,
[\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{0}}},0]
, and
[0,\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{1}}}]
under
+
and
\mspace{};\mspace{.5}
.

In the sequel, we will use the notation
\top
,
\bot
,
\land
,
\lor
for tests,
synonymously to
1
,
0
,
\mspace{};\mspace{.5}
,
+
to emphasize their logical character.

Lemma 11
.

Let
\mathbf{C}^{?}
be tests in an idempotent grove category
\mathbf{C}
with binary coproducts.

1.

The morphisms
\mathinner{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\scalebox{1.35}{$\diamond$}}}\colon\mathbf{C}^{?}(X)\to\mathbf{C}(X,X\oplus X)
,
{?\colon\mathbf{C}(X,X\oplus X)\to\mathbf{C}^{?}(X)}
defined by

\mathinner{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\scalebox{1.35}{$\diamond$}}}b=\bar{b}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{0}}}+b\mspace{};\mspace{.5}\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{1}}}
,
d?=d\mspace{};\mspace{.5}[0,1]

form a retraction.

2.

Every morphism
d
in the image of
\mathinner{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\scalebox{1.35}{$\diamond$}}}
is linear. Moreover, we have

d\mspace{};\mspace{.5}\nabla=1
,
d=d\mathrel{\texttt{\&\kern-0.5pt\&}}d
, and
d=d\mathrel{\texttt{|\kern-2.95pt|}}d
.

3.

For all
e
and
d
in the image of
\mathinner{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\scalebox{1.35}{$\diamond$}}}
, it holds that
(e\mathrel{\texttt{|\kern-2.95pt|}}d)?=e?\lor d?
,
(e\mathrel{\texttt{\&\kern-0.5pt\&}}d)?=e?\land d?
, and

(\mathrel{\texttt{{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\,\texttildelow}}}}\kern-1.5ptd)?=\overline{d?}
.

11
indicates that in presence of coproducts and with
linear coproduct injections, instead of Boolean algebras on subsets of
\mathbf{C}(X,X)
,
one can equivalently work with Boolean algebras on subsets of
\mathbf{C}(X,X\oplus X)
.

We conclude this section by an illustration that varying tests, in particular,
going beyond smallest expressive tests is practically advantageous.

Example 12
.

Consider the
nondeterministic state monad

{\bm{\mathsf{T}}}
with
TX={\mathcal{P}}(S\times X)^{S}

on
\mathbf{Set}
, where
S
is a fixed
global store
, which the programs, represented
by Kleisli morphisms of
{\bm{\mathsf{T}}}
are allowed to read and modify. Morphisms of the Kleisli category
\mathbf{Set}_{{\bm{\mathsf{T}}}}
are equivalently (by uncurrying)
maps of the form
p\colon S\times X\to{\mathcal{P}}(S\times Y)
, meaning that
\mathbf{Set}_{{\bm{\mathsf{T}}}}

is equivalent to a full subcategory of
\mathbf{Set}_{{\mathcal{P}}}
, from which
\mathbf{Set}_{{\bm{\mathsf{T}}}}
inherits the
structure of an idempotent grove category. The tests identified in
10

are those maps
b\colon S\times X\to{\mathcal{P}}(S\times X)
that are determined by decompositions
X=X_{1}\oplus X_{2}
,
in particular, they can neither read nor modify the store. In practice,
only the second is regarded as undesirable (and indeed would break commutativity of tests),
while reading is typically allowed. This leads to a more permissive notion
of tests, as those that are determined by the decompositions
S\times X=X_{1}\oplus X_{2}
.

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{0}}}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}[p,q]=p\qquad\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{1}}}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}[p,q]=q\qquad[\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{0}}},\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{1}}}]=1\qquad[p,q]\mspace{};\mspace{.5}r=[p\mspace{};\mspace{.5}r,q\mspace{};\mspace{.5}r]

\displaystyle 0+p=p\qquad p+p=p\qquad p+q=q+p\qquad(p+q)+r=p+(q+r)

\displaystyle 0\mspace{};\mspace{.5}p=0\qquad(q+r)\mspace{};\mspace{.5}p=q\mspace{};\mspace{.5}p+r\mspace{};\mspace{.5}p\qquad u\mspace{};\mspace{.5}0=0\qquad u\mspace{};\mspace{.5}(p+q)=u\mspace{};\mspace{.5}p+u\mspace{};\mspace{.5}q

\displaystyle(\text{{$\ast$-Fix}})~~p^{\ast}=1+p\mspace{};\mspace{.5}p^{\ast}\quad(\text{{$\ast$-Sum}})~~(p+q)^{\ast}=p^{\ast}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}(q\mspace{};\mspace{.5}p^{\ast})^{\ast}\quad(\text{{$\ast$-Uni}})~~\vbox{$u\mspace{};\mspace{.5}p^{\ast}=q^{\ast}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}uu\mspace{};\mspace{.5}p=q\mspace{};\mspace{.5}u$}

Figure 1:
Axioms of KiCs, including binary coproducts (
p,q,r
range over
\mathbf{C}
,
u
ranges over
\accentset{\rule{4.50697pt}{0.6pt}}{\mathbf{C}}
).

5
Kleene Iteration, Categorically

We now can introduce our central definition by extending idempotent grove categories with a selected class of linear morphisms,
called
tame morphisms
, and with Kleene iteration.
Crucially, we assume the ambient category
\mathbf{C}
to have coproducts as a necessary ingredient.
Finding a general definition, not relying on coproducts, presently remains open.

Definition 13

(KiC(T))
.

We call a tuple
(\mathbf{C},\accentset{\rule{4.50697pt}{0.6pt}}{\mathbf{C}})
a
Kleene-iteration category (KiC)

if

1.

\mathbf{C}
is an idempotent grove category with coproducts;

2.

\accentset{\rule{4.50697pt}{0.6pt}}{\mathbf{C}}
is a wide subcategory of
\mathbf{C}
, whose morphisms we call
tame

such that

•

\accentset{\rule{4.50697pt}{0.6pt}}{\mathbf{C}}
has coproducts strictly preserved by the inclusion to
\mathbf{C}
;

•

the morphisms of
\accentset{\rule{4.50697pt}{0.6pt}}{\mathbf{C}}
are all linear;

3.

for every
X\in|\mathbf{C}|
, there is a
Kleene iteration
operator
(\operatorname{-\!-})^{\ast}\colon\mathbf{C}(X,X)\to\mathbf{C}(X,X)

such that the laws
\ast
-Fix
,
\ast
-Sum
and
\ast
-Uni
in
Figure 1
, with
u
ranging over
\accentset{\rule{4.50697pt}{0.6pt}}{\mathbf{C}}
, are satisfied.

A functor
F\colon(\mathbf{C},\accentset{\rule{4.50697pt}{0.6pt}}{\mathbf{C}})\to(\mathbf{D},\accentset{\rule{4.50697pt}{0.6pt}}{\mathbf{D}})
between KiCs
is a coproduct preserving functor
{F\colon\mathbf{C}\to\mathbf{D}}
such that
F0=0
,
F(q+r)=Fq+Fr
and
Fp^{\ast}=(Fp)^{\ast}

for all
q,r\in\mathbf{C}(X,Y)
,
p\in\mathbf{C}(X,X)
, and
Fp\in\accentset{\rule{4.50697pt}{0.6pt}}{\mathbf{D}}(FX,FY)
for all
p\in\accentset{\rule{4.50697pt}{0.6pt}}{\mathbf{C}}(X,Y)
.

A KiC
(\mathbf{C},\accentset{\rule{4.50697pt}{0.6pt}}{\mathbf{C}})
equipped with a choice of tests
\mathbf{C}^{?}
in
\accentset{\rule{4.50697pt}{0.6pt}}{\mathbf{C}}
we call a
KiCT
(=KiC with tests).
Correspondingly, functors between KiCTs are additionally required to send tests to tests.

It transpires from the definition that the role of tameness is to limit the
power of the uniformity rule
\ast
-Uni
. The principal case for
\mathbf{C}\neq\accentset{\rule{4.50697pt}{0.6pt}}{\mathbf{C}}

is
2
. As we see later (
24
), this yields a KiC.
More generally, unless we restrict
\accentset{\rule{4.50697pt}{0.6pt}}{\mathbf{C}}
to programs that satisfy the linearity laws (
4
), the uniformity principle
\ast
-Uni
would tend to be unsound. Very roughly, uniformity is some infinitary form of distributivity from the left and it fails for programs that fail the standard left distributivity. This phenomenon is expected to occur for other flavors of concurrent semantics: as long as
\mathbf{C}
admits morphisms that fail (
4
),
\accentset{\rule{4.50697pt}{0.6pt}}{\mathbf{C}}
would have to be properly smaller than
\mathbf{C}
. Apart from concurrency, if
\accentset{\rule{4.50697pt}{0.6pt}}{\mathbf{C}}
models a language with exceptions, those must be excluded from
\accentset{\rule{4.50697pt}{0.6pt}}{\mathbf{C}}
, for otherwise uniformity would again become unsound.

If we demand all morphisms
to be tame, we will obtain a notion very close to that of Kleene-Kozen category (
3
).

Definition 14

(
\ast
-Idempotence)
.

A KiC is
\ast
-idempotent
if it satisfies (
2
).

Proposition 15
.

A category
\mathbf{C}
is Kleene-Kozen
iff
(\mathbf{C},\mathbf{C})
is a
\ast
-idempotent KiC.

Proof.

As shown previously
[
16
]
,
\mathbf{C}
is a Kleene-Kozen category iff

1.

\mathbf{C}
is enriched over bounded join-semilattices and strict join-preserving morphisms;

2.

there is an operator
(\operatorname{-\!-})^{\ast}\colon\mathbf{C}(X,X)\to\mathbf{C}(X,X)

such that

(a)

p^{\ast}=1+p\mspace{};\mspace{.5}p^{\ast}
;

(b)

1^{\ast}=1
;

(c)

p^{\ast}=(p+1)^{\ast}
;

(d)

u\mspace{};\mspace{.5}p=q\mspace{};\mspace{.5}u
implies
u\mspace{};\mspace{.5}p^{\ast}=q^{\ast}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}u
.

This yields sufficiency by noting that (1) states precisely that all morphisms
in
\mathbf{C}
are linear. To show necessity, it suffices to obtain (2.c) from the assumptions
that
(\mathbf{C},\mathbf{C})
is a
\ast
-idempotent KiC and that all morphisms in
\mathbf{C}
are
linear. Indeed, we have
(p+1)^{\ast}=1^{\ast}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}(p\mspace{};\mspace{.5}1^{\ast})^{\ast}=p^{\ast}
.∎

KiCs thus deviate from Kleene algebras precisely in four respects:

1.

by generalizing from monoids to categories,

2.

by allowing non-linear morphisms,

3.

by dropping
\ast
-idempotence, and

4.

by requiring binary coproducts.

Example 16
.

The axiom
\ast
-Sum
, included in
13
, is one of the
classical
Conway identities
. The other one

(p\mspace{};\mspace{.5}q)^{\ast}=1+p\mspace{};\mspace{.5}(q\mspace{};\mspace{.5}p)^{\ast}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}q

is derivable if
\mathbf{C}=\accentset{\rule{4.50697pt}{0.6pt}}{\mathbf{C}}
, e.g. in Kleene-Kozen categories.
Indeed,
q\mspace{};\mspace{.5}p\mspace{};\mspace{.5}q=q\mspace{};\mspace{.5}p\mspace{};\mspace{.5}q
entails
q\mspace{};\mspace{.5}(p\mspace{};\mspace{.5}q)^{\ast}=(q\mspace{};\mspace{.5}p)^{\ast}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}q

by
\ast
-Uni
, and using
\ast
-Fix
,
(p\mspace{};\mspace{.5}q)^{\ast}=1+p\mspace{};\mspace{.5}q\mspace{};\mspace{.5}(p\mspace{};\mspace{.5}q)^{\ast}=1+p\mspace{};\mspace{.5}(q\mspace{};\mspace{.5}p)^{\ast}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}q
.

Clearly, this argument remains valid with only
q
being tame, but otherwise
the requisite identity is not provable.

It may not be obvious why the requirement to support binary coproducts is part of

13
, given that the axioms of iteration do not involve them. The reason is that
certain identities that also do not involve coproducts are only derivable in their presence.

Example 17
.

The identity
p^{\ast}=(p\mspace{};\mspace{.5}(1+p))^{\ast}
holds in
any KiC.

A standard way to instantiate
13
is to start with a category

\mathbf{V}
with coproducts, and a monad
{\bm{\mathsf{T}}}
on it, and take
\mathbf{C}=\mathbf{V}_{{\bm{\mathsf{T}}}}
,

\accentset{\rule{4.50697pt}{0.6pt}}{\mathbf{C}}=\mathbf{V}
or, possibly,
\accentset{\rule{4.50697pt}{0.6pt}}{\mathbf{C}}=\mathbf{V}_{{\bm{\mathsf{T}}}}
. The monad must support nondeterminism and Kleene iteration
so that the axioms of KiC are satisfied. Consider a class of Kleene-Kozen categories
that arise in this way.

Example 18
.

Let
Q
be a unital quantale, and let
TX=Q^{X}
for every set
X
. Then
T
extends
to a monad on
\mathbf{Set}
as follows:
\eta(x)(x)=1
,
\eta(x)(y)=\bot
if
x\neq y
, and

(p\colon X\to Q^{Y})^{\sharp}(f\colon X\to Q)(y\in Y)=\bigvee_{x\in X}p(x)(y)\cdot f(x).

We obtain a Kleene-Kozen structure in
\mathbf{Set}_{{\bm{\mathsf{T}}}}
as follows:

•

0\colon X\to Q^{Y}
sends
x
to
\lambda y.\,\bot
;

•

p+q\colon X\to Q^{Y}
sends
x
to
\lambda y.\,p(x)(y)\lor q(x)(y)
;

•

p^{\ast}\colon X\to Q^{X}
is the least fixpoint of the map
q\mapsto 1+q\mspace{};\mspace{.5}p
.

This construction restricts to
Q^{X}_{\omega_{1}}=\{f\colon X\to Q\mid|\operatorname{\mathsf{supp}}f|\leq\omega\}

where
{\operatorname{\mathsf{supp}}f}
is the set of those
x\in X
, for which
f(x)\neq 0
. Thus,
e.g. the Kleisli categories of the powerset monad
{\mathcal{P}}
and the countable
powerset monad
{\mathcal{P}}_{\omega_{1}}
are Kleene-Kozen.

For a contrast, consider a similar construction that yields a KiC, which
is not Kleene-Kozen.

Example 19
.

Let
Q=\{0,1,\infty\}
be the complete lattice under the ordering
0<1<\infty
,
and let us define commutative binary multiplication as follows:
0\cdot x=0
,

1\cdot x=x
and
\infty\cdot\infty=\infty
. This turns
Q
into a unital quantale,
hence an idempotent semiring, whose binary summation
+
is binary join.
Next, define infinite summation with the formula

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in I}x_{i}=\begin{cases}\bigvee_{i\in I^{\prime}}x_{i},&\text{if $I^{\prime}=\{i\in I\mid x_{i}>0\}$ is finite}\\
\infty,&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

This makes
Q
into a
complete semiring

[
11
]
. Let us define
the monad
\mathcal{R}
and the idempotent grove structure on
\mathbf{Set}_{\mathcal{R}}
like
Q^{(\operatorname{-\!-})}_{\omega_{1}}
in
18
(with
\sum
instead of
\bigvee
).
For every
{f\colon X\to\mathcal{R}X}
, let
p^{\ast}=\sum_{n\in{\rm Nature}}p^{n}\colon X\to\mathcal{R}X
where,
inductively,
p^{0}=1
and
p^{n+1}=p\mspace{};\mspace{.5}p^{n}
, and infinite sums are extended
from
Q
to the Kleisli hom-sets pointwise.

It is easy to verify that
(\mathbf{Set}_{\mathcal{R}},\mathbf{Set}_{\mathcal{R}})
is a KiC, but

\mathbf{Set}_{\mathcal{R}}
is not a Kleene-Kozen category, for
\ast
-idempotence fails:

\eta^{\ast}=\sum_{n\in{\rm Nature}}\eta^{n}=\sum_{n\in{\rm Nature}}\eta=\lambda x,y.\,\infty\neq\eta
.
We will use a more convenient notation for the elements of
\mathcal{R}X
as infinite formal sums

\sum_{i\in I}x_{i}
(
x_{i}\in X
), modulo associativity, commutativity, idempotence
(but without countable idempotence
\sum_{i\in I}x=x
!).

Below, we provide two results for constructing new KiCs from
old:
Theorem 20
and
Theorem 23
, which are also used prominently in our characterization result in
Section 7
.

Theorem 20
.

Let
(\mathbf{C},\accentset{\rule{4.50697pt}{0.6pt}}{\mathbf{C}})
be a KiC and let
{\bm{\mathsf{T}}}
be a
monad on
\mathbf{C}
such that

1.

Tr^{\ast}=(Tr)^{\ast}
,
for all
r\in\mathbf{C}(X,X)
;

2.

the monad
{\bm{\mathsf{T}}}
restricts to a monad on
\accentset{\rule{4.50697pt}{0.6pt}}{\mathbf{C}}
.

Then
\accentset{\rule{4.50697pt}{0.6pt}}{\mathbf{C}}_{\bm{\mathsf{T}}}
is a subcategory of
\accentset{\rule{4.50697pt}{0.6pt}}{\mathbf{C}}
and
(\mathbf{C}_{\bm{\mathsf{T}}},\accentset{\rule{4.50697pt}{0.6pt}}{\mathbf{C}}_{\bm{\mathsf{T}}})
is a KiCT where
0
and

+
are defined as in
\mathbf{C}
, and for any
p\colon X\to TX
, the corresponding Kleene
iteration is computed as
\eta\mspace{};\mspace{.5}(p^{\sharp})^{\ast}
.

Let us illustrate the use of
Theorem 20
by a simple example.

Example 21

(Finite Traces)
.

Consider the monad
{\mathcal{P}}(A^{\star}\times\operatorname{-\!-})
on
\mathbf{Set}
. Elements of

{\mathcal{P}}(A^{\star}\times X)
are standardly used as (finite) trace semantics
of programs. A trace is then a sequence of actions from
A
, followed by an end result
in
X
. Of course, it can be verified directly that the Kleisli category of
{\mathcal{P}}(A^{\star}\times\operatorname{-\!-})

is Kleene-Kozen. Let us show how this follows from
Theorem 20
.

The Kleisli category of
{\mathcal{P}}
is isomorphic to the category of relations, and is
obviously Kleene-Kozen. For
{\mathcal{P}}
, like for any commutative monad, the Kleisli category

\mathbf{Set}_{{\mathcal{P}}}
is symmetric monoidal:
X\mathbin{\otimes}Y=X\times Y
and, given

p\colon X\to{\mathcal{P}}Y
,
q\colon X^{\prime}\to{\mathcal{P}}Y^{\prime}
,

(p\mathbin{\otimes}q)(x,x^{\prime})=\{(y,y^{\prime})\mid y\in p(x),\operatorname{}\linebreak y^{\prime}\in q(x^{\prime})\}.

The set
A^{\star}
is a monoid in
\mathbf{Set}_{{\mathcal{P}}}
w.r.t. this monoidal structure. This
yields a writer monad
{\bm{\mathsf{T}}}
on
\mathbf{Set}_{{\mathcal{P}}}
via
TX=A^{\star}\mathbin{\otimes}X
and
(Tp)(w,x)=\{(w,y)\mid y\in p(x)\}
. Its Kleisli category

(\mathbf{Set}_{{\mathcal{P}}})_{{\bm{\mathsf{T}}}}
is isomorphic to our original Kleisli category of interest. The assumptions (1)
of
Theorem 20
are thus satisfied in the obvious way. The assumption (2)
is vacuous, as we chose all morphisms to be tame.

Finally, we establish robustness of KiCs under the

generalized coalgebraic resumption monad transformer

[
35
,
17
]
, which is defined as follows.

Definition 22

(Coalgebraic Resumptions)
.

Let
\mathbf{V}
be a category with coproducts and let
{\bm{\mathsf{T}}}
be a monad on
\mathbf{V}
.
Let
H\colon\mathbf{V}\to\mathbf{V}
be some endofunctor and assume that all final coalgebras
\nu\gamma.\,T(X\oplus H\gamma)

exist. The assignment
X\mapsto\nu\gamma.\,T(X\oplus H\gamma)
yields a monad
{\bm{\mathsf{T}}}_{H}
, called
the (generalized) coalgebraic resumption monad transformer of
{\bm{\mathsf{T}}}
.

Theorem 23
.

Let
{\bm{\mathsf{T}}}_{H}
be as in
22
and such that
(\mathbf{V}_{{\bm{\mathsf{T}}}},\accentset{\rule{4.50697pt}{0.6pt}}{\mathbf{C}})

is a KiC for some choice of
\accentset{\rule{4.50697pt}{0.6pt}}{\mathbf{C}}
. Then
\mathbf{V}_{{\bm{\mathsf{T}}}}
is a
wide subcategory of
\mathbf{V}_{{\bm{\mathsf{T}}}_{H}}
and
(\mathbf{V}_{{\bm{\mathsf{T}}}_{H}},\accentset{\rule{4.50697pt}{0.6pt}}{\mathbf{C}})

is a KiC w.r.t. the following structure:

•

the bottom element in every
\mathbf{V}(X,T_{H}Y)
is
0\mspace{};\mspace{.5}\mathsf{out}^{\text{\kern 0.5pt-}\mathsf{\kern-0.5pt1}}
,
and the join of
p,q\in\mathbf{V}(X,T_{H}Y)
is
(p\mspace{};\mspace{.5}\mathsf{out}+q\mspace{};\mspace{.5}\mathsf{out})\mspace{};\mspace{.5}\mathsf{out}^{\text{\kern 0.5pt-}\mathsf{\kern-0.5pt1}}
;

•

given
p\in\mathbf{V}(X,T_{H}X)
,
p^{\ast}\in\mathbf{V}(X,T_{H}X)
is the unique
solution of the equation

p^{\ast}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}\mathsf{out}=\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{0}}}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}[p\mspace{};\mspace{.5}\mathsf{out},0]^{\ast}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}T(1\oplus Hp^{\ast}).

We defer the proof to
Section 6
where we use reduction to the existing
result
[
19
]
, using the equivalence of Kleene and Elgot iterations,
we establish in
Section 6
.

Example 24
.

By taking
T={\mathcal{P}}_{\omega_{1}}
and
H=A\times\operatorname{-\!-}
in
Theorem 23
, we obtain
T_{H}X=\nu\gamma.\,{\mathcal{P}}_{\omega_{1}}(X\oplus A\times\gamma)

from
2
. Let us illustrate the effect of Kleene iteration by example.
Consider the system of equations

\displaystyle P=a.\,P+Q,\qquad Q=b.\,Q+P

for defining the behaviour of two processes
P
and
Q
. This system induces a
function
p\colon\{P,Q\}\to T_{H}\{P,Q\}
, sending
P
to
a.\,P+Q
and
Q
to
b.\,Q+P
.
The expression
[p\mspace{};\mspace{.5}\mathsf{out},0]^{\ast}
calls the iteration operator of the powerset-monad,
resulting in the function that sends both
P
and
Q
to
a.\,P+b.\,Q+Q+P
.
Finally,
p^{\ast}
sends
P
to
P^{\prime}
and
Q
to
Q^{\prime}
, where
P^{\prime}
and
Q^{\prime}

are the synchronization trees, obtained as unique solutions of the system:

\displaystyle P^{\prime}=a.\,P^{\prime}+b.\,Q^{\prime}+Q+P,\qquad Q=a.\,P^{\prime}+b.\,Q^{\prime}+Q+P.

6
Elgot Iteration and While-Loops

In this section, we establish an equivalence between Kleene iteration, in the
sense of KiC and
Elgot iteration
, as an operation with the following
profile in a category
\mathbf{C}
with coproducts:

\displaystyle(\operatorname{-\!-})^{\dagger}\colon\mathbf{C}(X,Y\oplus X)\to\mathbf{C}(X,Y).

(7)

This could be done directly, but we prove an equivalence between Elgot
iteration and while-loops first, and then prove the equivalence of the latter and
Kleene iteration. In this chain of equivalences, only while-loops need tests,
and it will follow that a particular choice of tests is not
relevant, once they are expressive. On the other hand, existence of expressive
tests is guaranteed by
10
. This explains why tests disappear
in the resulting equivalence.

Definition 25

(Conway Iteration, Uniformity)
.

An Elgot iteration operator (
7
) in a category
\mathbf{C}
with coproducts is

Conway iteration

[
13
]
if it satisfies the following
principles:

\displaystyle\text{{Naturality}}:

\displaystyle~~p^{\dagger}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}q=(p\mspace{};\mspace{.5}(q\oplus 1))^{\dagger}

\displaystyle~\text{{Dinaturality}}:~(p\mspace{};\mspace{.5}[\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{0}}},q])^{\dagger}=p\mspace{};\mspace{.5}[1,(q\mspace{};\mspace{.5}[\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{0}}},p])^{\dagger}]

\displaystyle\text{{Codiagonal}}:

\displaystyle~~(p\mspace{};\mspace{.5}[1,\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{1}}}])^{\dagger}=p^{\dagger\dagger}

Moreover, given a subcategory
\mathbf{D}
of
\mathbf{C}
,
(\operatorname{-\!-})^{\dagger}
is
uniform

w.r.t.
\mathbf{D}
, or
\mathbf{D}
-uniform, if it satisfies

\displaystyle\text{{Uniformity}}:

\displaystyle\quad\textbf{}~~\frac{u\mspace{};\mspace{.5}q=p\mspace{};\mspace{.5}(1\oplus u)}{u\mspace{};\mspace{.5}q^{\dagger}=p^{\dagger}}\qquad\text{(with $u$ from $\mathbf{D}$)}

By taking
q=\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{1}}}
in
Dinaturality
, we derive

\displaystyle\text{{Fixpoint}}:p\mspace{};\mspace{.5}[1,p^{\dagger}]=p^{\dagger}.

DW-Fix:

\displaystyle\quad\operatorname{\mathsf{\underline{while}}}\mathbin{\,}d\mathbin{}\operatorname{\mathsf{\underline{do}}}\mathbin{\,}p=\operatorname{\mathsf{\underline{if}}}\mathbin{}d\operatorname{\mathsf{\underline{then}}}\mathbin{}p\mspace{};\mspace{.5}(\operatorname{\mathsf{\underline{while}}}\mathbin{\,}d\mathbin{}\operatorname{\mathsf{\underline{do}}}\mathbin{\,}p)\operatorname{\mathsf{\underline{else}}}\mathbin{\,}1

DW-Or:

\displaystyle\quad\operatorname{\mathsf{\underline{while}}}\mathbin{\,}(d\mathrel{\texttt{|\kern-2.95pt|}}e)\mathbin{}\operatorname{\mathsf{\underline{do}}}\mathbin{\,}p=(\operatorname{\mathsf{\underline{while}}}\mathbin{\,}d\mathbin{}\operatorname{\mathsf{\underline{do}}}\mathbin{\,}p)\mspace{};\mspace{.5}\operatorname{\mathsf{\underline{while}}}\mathbin{\,}e\mathbin{}\operatorname{\mathsf{\underline{do}}}\mathbin{\,}(p\mspace{};\mspace{.5}\operatorname{\mathsf{\underline{while}}}\mathbin{\,}d\mathbin{}\operatorname{\mathsf{\underline{do}}}\mathbin{\,}p)

DW-And:

\displaystyle\quad\operatorname{\mathsf{\underline{while}}}\mathbin{\,}(d\mathrel{\texttt{\&\kern-0.5pt\&}}(e\mathrel{\texttt{|\kern-2.95pt|}}\operatorname{\mathsf{t\kern-0.45ptt}}))\mathbin{}\operatorname{\mathsf{\underline{do}}}\mathbin{\,}p=\operatorname{\mathsf{\underline{while}}}\mathbin{\,}d\mathbin{}\operatorname{\mathsf{\underline{do}}}\mathbin{\,}(\operatorname{\mathsf{\underline{if}}}\mathbin{}e\operatorname{\mathsf{\underline{then}}}\mathbin{}p\operatorname{\mathsf{\underline{else}}}\mathbin{\,}p)\qquad

DW-Uni:

\displaystyle\quad\frac{u\mspace{};\mspace{.5}\operatorname{\mathsf{\underline{if}}}\mathbin{}d\operatorname{\mathsf{\underline{then}}}\mathbin{}p\mspace{};\mspace{.5}\operatorname{\mathsf{t\kern-0.45ptt}}\operatorname{\mathsf{\underline{else}}}\mathbin{\,}\operatorname{\mathsf{f\kern-0.78ptf}}=\operatorname{\mathsf{\underline{if}}}\mathbin{}e\operatorname{\mathsf{\underline{then}}}\mathbin{}q\mspace{};\mspace{.5}u\mspace{};\mspace{.5}\operatorname{\mathsf{t\kern-0.45ptt}}\operatorname{\mathsf{\underline{else}}}\mathbin{\,}v\mspace{};\mspace{.5}\operatorname{\mathsf{f\kern-0.78ptf}}}{u\mspace{};\mspace{.5}\operatorname{\mathsf{\underline{while}}}\mathbin{\,}d\mathbin{}\operatorname{\mathsf{\underline{do}}}\mathbin{\,}p=(\operatorname{\mathsf{\underline{while}}}\mathbin{\,}e\mathbin{}\operatorname{\mathsf{\underline{do}}}\mathbin{\,}q)\mspace{};\mspace{.5}v}

Figure 2:
Uniform Conway iteration in terms of decisions.

Theorem 26
.

Let
\mathbf{C}
be a category with coproducts, let
\mathbf{D}
be its wide subcategory with
coproducts, preserved by the inclusion, and let for every

{X\in|\mathbf{C}|}
,
\mathbf{C}^{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\scalebox{.95}{$\diamond$}}}(X)
be a set of decisions such that
(i)
\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{0}}},\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{1}}}\in\mathbf{C}^{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\scalebox{.95}{$\diamond$}}}(X)
, (ii)
\mathbf{C}^{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\scalebox{.95}{$\diamond$}}}(X)
is closed under (
5
),
(iii)
\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{0}}}\oplus\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{1}}}\in\mathbf{C}^{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\scalebox{.95}{$\diamond$}}}(X)
if
X=X_{1}\oplus X_{2}
.
Then, to give a
\mathbf{D}
-uniform Conway iteration on
\mathbf{C}
is the
same as to give an operator

\displaystyle\frac{d\in\mathbf{C}^{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\scalebox{.95}{$\diamond$}}}(X)\qquad p\in\mathbf{C}(X,X)}{\operatorname{\mathsf{\underline{while}}}\mathbin{\,}d\mathbin{}\operatorname{\mathsf{\underline{do}}}\mathbin{\,}p\in\mathbf{C}(X,X)}

that satisfies the laws in
Figure 2
with
p,q
ranging over
\mathbf{C}
, and with
u,v
ranging over
\mathbf{D}
.

Theorem 26
yields an equivalence between two styles of iteration:
Elgot iteration and while-iteration. We next specialize it to idempotent grove categories
with tests using
11
.

Note that in any KiC
(\mathbf{C},\accentset{\rule{4.50697pt}{0.6pt}}{\mathbf{C}})
with tests
\mathbf{C}^{?}
, in addition to
the if-then-else (
6
), we have the while operator, defined in the standard way:
given
b\in\mathbf{C}^{?}(X)
,
p\in\mathbf{C}(X,X)
,

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathsf{while}}\mathbin{}b\operatorname{\mathsf{do}}\mathbin{}p=(b\mspace{};\mspace{.5}p)^{\ast}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}\bar{b}.

(8)

Proposition 27
.

Let
\mathbf{C}
be an idempotent grove category, let
\mathbf{D}
be a wide subcategory of
\mathbf{C}
with
coproducts, which are preserved by the inclusion to
\mathbf{C}
, and with expressive tests

\mathbf{C}^{?}
. Then
\mathbf{C}
supports
\mathbf{D}
-uniform Conway
iteration iff it supports a while-operator
that satisfies the laws in
Figure 3
, where
b
and
c
come from

\mathbf{C}^{?}
,
p
and
q
come from
\mathbf{C}
and
u,v
come from
\mathbf{D}
and the if-then-else operator is defined as in (
8
).

Proof.

For every
X\in|\mathbf{C}|
, let
\mathbf{C}^{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\scalebox{.95}{$\diamond$}}}(X)
be the image of
\mathbf{C}^{?}(X)
under
\mathinner{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\scalebox{1.35}{$\diamond$}}}
from
11
.
As shown in the lemma,
\mathbf{C}^{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\scalebox{.95}{$\diamond$}}}(X)
inherits the Boolean algebra structure from
\mathbf{C}^{?}(X)
.
Using the isomorphism between
\mathbf{C}^{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\scalebox{.95}{$\diamond$}}}(X)
and
\mathbf{C}^{?}(X)
and
7
, the laws
from
Figure 2
can be reformulated equivalently, resulting in
TW-Fix
,

TW-Or
,
TW-Uni
, and additionally

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathsf{while}}\mathbin{}(b\land(c\lor\top))\operatorname{\mathsf{do}}\mathbin{}p=

\displaystyle\;\operatorname{\mathsf{while}}\mathbin{}b\operatorname{\mathsf{do}}\mathbin{}(\operatorname{\mathsf{if}}\mathbin{}c\operatorname{\mathsf{then}}\mathbin{}p\operatorname{\mathsf{else}}\mathbin{}p)

which however holds trivially.
∎

Thus, in grove categories with expressive
tests, Elgot iteration and while-loops are equivalent. We establish a
similar equivalence between Kleene iteration and while-loops, which will
entail an equivalence between Elgot iteration and Kleene iteration by transitivity.

Theorem 28
.

Let
\mathbf{C}
,
\accentset{\rule{4.50697pt}{0.6pt}}{\mathbf{C}}
and
\mathbf{C}^{?}
be as follows.

1.

\mathbf{C}
is an idempotent grove category with coproducts.

2.

\accentset{\rule{4.50697pt}{0.6pt}}{\mathbf{C}}
is a wide subcategory of
\mathbf{C}
with coproducts,
consisting of linear morphisms only and such that the inclusion of
\accentset{\rule{4.50697pt}{0.6pt}}{\mathbf{C}}
to
\mathbf{C}
preserves coproducts.

3.

\mathbf{C}^{?}
are expressive tests in
\mathbf{C}
.

Then
(\mathbf{C},\accentset{\rule{4.50697pt}{0.6pt}}{\mathbf{C}})
is a KiCT iff
\mathbf{C}
supports a while-operator
satisfying the laws in
Figure 3
.

We can now characterize KiCs in terms of Elgot iteration.

Theorem 29
.

Let
\mathbf{C}
be an idempotent grove category with coproducts, and let
\accentset{\rule{4.50697pt}{0.6pt}}{\mathbf{C}}
be
a wide subcategory of
\mathbf{C}
with coproducts, consisting of linear
morphisms only and such that the inclusion of
\accentset{\rule{4.50697pt}{0.6pt}}{\mathbf{C}}
to
\mathbf{C}
preserves coproducts.

Then
(\mathbf{C},\accentset{\rule{4.50697pt}{0.6pt}}{\mathbf{C}})
is a KiC iff
\mathbf{C}
supports
\accentset{\rule{4.50697pt}{0.6pt}}{\mathbf{C}}
-uniform
Conway iteration.

Proof.

Let us define
\mathbf{C}^{?}
as in
9
. By
Theorem 28
,

(\mathbf{C},\accentset{\rule{4.50697pt}{0.6pt}}{\mathbf{C}})
is a KiCT iff
\mathbf{C}
supports a while-operator, satisfying the laws in
Figure 3
. By
27
,
the latter is the case iff
\mathbf{C}
supports
\accentset{\rule{4.50697pt}{0.6pt}}{\mathbf{C}}
-uniform Conway
iteration.
∎

TW-Fix:

\displaystyle\quad\operatorname{\mathsf{while}}\mathbin{}b\operatorname{\mathsf{do}}\mathbin{}p=\operatorname{\mathsf{if}}\mathbin{}b\operatorname{\mathsf{then}}\mathbin{}p\mspace{};\mspace{.5}(\operatorname{\mathsf{while}}\mathbin{}b\operatorname{\mathsf{do}}\mathbin{}p)\operatorname{\mathsf{else}}\mathbin{}1

TW-Or:

\displaystyle\quad\operatorname{\mathsf{while}}\mathbin{}(b\lor c)\operatorname{\mathsf{do}}\mathbin{}p=(\operatorname{\mathsf{while}}\mathbin{}b\operatorname{\mathsf{do}}\mathbin{}p)\mspace{};\mspace{.5}\operatorname{\mathsf{while}}\mathbin{}c\operatorname{\mathsf{do}}\mathbin{}(p\mspace{};\mspace{.5}\operatorname{\mathsf{while}}\mathbin{}b\operatorname{\mathsf{do}}\mathbin{}p)\qquad

TW-Uni:

\displaystyle\quad\frac{u\mspace{};\mspace{.5}\bar{b}=\bar{c}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}v\qquad u\mspace{};\mspace{.5}b\mspace{};\mspace{.5}p=c\mspace{};\mspace{.5}q\mspace{};\mspace{.5}u}{u\mspace{};\mspace{.5}\operatorname{\mathsf{while}}\mathbin{}b\operatorname{\mathsf{do}}\mathbin{}p=(\operatorname{\mathsf{while}}\mathbin{}c\operatorname{\mathsf{do}}\mathbin{}q)\mspace{};\mspace{.5}v}

Figure 3:
Uniform Conway iteration in terms of tests.

Now we can prove
Theorem 23
.

Proof
Theorem 23
(Sketch).

We need to check that
{(\mathbf{V}_{{\bm{\mathsf{T}}}_{H}},\accentset{\rule{4.50697pt}{0.6pt}}{\mathbf{C}})}

is a KiC. By
Theorem 29
, we equivalently prove that

\mathbf{V}_{{\bm{\mathsf{T}}}_{H}}
supports
\accentset{\rule{4.50697pt}{0.6pt}}{\mathbf{C}}
-uniform Conway iteration.
It is already known
[
19
, Lemma 7.2]
that if
{\bm{\mathsf{T}}}
supports Conway iteration,
then so does
{\bm{\mathsf{T}}}_{H}
.
By
Theorem 29
, we are left to check that
{\bm{\mathsf{T}}}_{H}
satisfies
Uniformity
,
which is a matter of calculation.
∎

7
Free KiCTs and Completeness

In this section, we characterize a free KiCT with strict coproducts (i.e. those,
for which coherence maps
X\oplus(Y\oplus Z)\mathbin{\cong}(X\oplus Y)\oplus Z
are identities) on a one-sorted
signature. We achieve this by combining techniques from formal languages
[
7
]
,
category theory and the theory of Elgot iteration with coalgebraic reasoning
[
37
]
, in particular proofs by coalgebraic bisimilarity.
We claim that a more general characterization of a free KiCT on a multi-sorted signature can be achieved
along the same lines, modulo a significant notation overhead and the necessity to form
final coalgebras in the category of multisorted sets
\mathbf{Set}^{\mathcal{S}}
where
\mathcal{S}
is the set of sorts.
We dispense with this option for the sake of brevity and readability.
Let us fix

•

a signatures of
n
-ary symbols
\Sigma_{n}
for each
n\in{\rm Nature}
, and let
\Sigma=\bigcup_{n}\Sigma_{n}
;

•

a signature
\Gamma
of (unary) symbols, disjoint from
\Sigma
;

•

a finite (!) signature
\Theta
of (unary) symbols, disjoint from
\Sigma\cup\Gamma
.

Let

\widehat{\phantom{\rule{8.0pt}{10.93333pt}}}

\textstyle\Theta

denote the set of finite subsets of
\Theta
.
We regard
\Theta
as a signature for tests,
\Gamma
as a signature for tame morphisms
and
\Sigma
as a signature for general morphisms;

\widehat{\phantom{\rule{8.0pt}{10.93333pt}}}

\textstyle\Theta

is meant to capture
finite conjunctions of the form
\mathsf{b}_{1}\land\ldots\land\mathsf{b}_{n}\land\bar{\mathsf{b}}_{n+1}\land\ldots\land\bar{\mathsf{b}}_{m}

as semantic correspondents of subsets
\{\mathsf{b}_{1},\ldots,\mathsf{b}_{n}\}\in\mathchoice{\mathchoice{{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\displaystyle\kern 0.15pt\scalebox{.8}[.65]{$\widehat{\phantom{\rule{8.0pt}{11.27492pt}}}$}$\hfil\cr\hfil$\displaystyle\Theta$\hfil\cr}}}}{{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\textstyle\kern 0.15pt\scalebox{.8}[.65]{$\widehat{\phantom{\rule{8.0pt}{11.27492pt}}}$}$\hfil\cr\hfil$\textstyle\Theta$\hfil\cr}}}}{{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\scriptstyle\kern 0.15pt\scalebox{.8}[.65]{$\widehat{\phantom{\rule{8.0pt}{11.27492pt}}}$}$\hfil\cr\hfil$\scriptstyle\Theta$\hfil\cr}}}}{{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\scriptscriptstyle\kern 0.15pt\scalebox{.8}[.65]{$\widehat{\phantom{\rule{8.0pt}{11.27492pt}}}$}$\hfil\cr\hfil$\scriptscriptstyle\Theta$\hfil\cr}}}}}{\mathchoice{{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\displaystyle\kern 0.15pt\scalebox{.8}[.65]{$\widehat{\phantom{\rule{8.0pt}{10.93333pt}}}$}$\hfil\cr\hfil$\displaystyle\Theta$\hfil\cr}}}}{{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\textstyle\kern 0.15pt\scalebox{.8}[.65]{$\widehat{\phantom{\rule{8.0pt}{10.93333pt}}}$}$\hfil\cr\hfil$\textstyle\Theta$\hfil\cr}}}}{{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\scriptstyle\kern 0.15pt\scalebox{.8}[.65]{$\widehat{\phantom{\rule{8.0pt}{10.93333pt}}}$}$\hfil\cr\hfil$\scriptstyle\Theta$\hfil\cr}}}}{{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\scriptscriptstyle\kern 0.15pt\scalebox{.8}[.65]{$\widehat{\phantom{\rule{8.0pt}{10.93333pt}}}$}$\hfil\cr\hfil$\scriptscriptstyle\Theta$\hfil\cr}}}}}{\widehat{\Theta}}{\widehat{\Theta}}
, assuming an enumeration

\Theta=\{\mathsf{b}_{1},\ldots,\mathsf{b}_{m}\}
. This is inspired
by Kleene algebra with tests
[
26
]
. Furthermore, we accommodate

guarded strings
from
op. cit.
: let

\Gamma^{\Theta}

be the set of strings

\langle b_{1},\mathsf{u}_{1},\ldots,b_{n},\mathsf{u}_{n},b_{n+1}\rangle

with
\mathsf{u}_{i}\in\Gamma
,
b_{i}\in\mathchoice{\mathchoice{{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\displaystyle\kern 0.15pt\scalebox{.8}[.65]{$\widehat{\phantom{\rule{8.0pt}{11.27492pt}}}$}$\hfil\cr\hfil$\displaystyle\Theta$\hfil\cr}}}}{{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\textstyle\kern 0.15pt\scalebox{.8}[.65]{$\widehat{\phantom{\rule{8.0pt}{11.27492pt}}}$}$\hfil\cr\hfil$\textstyle\Theta$\hfil\cr}}}}{{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\scriptstyle\kern 0.15pt\scalebox{.8}[.65]{$\widehat{\phantom{\rule{8.0pt}{11.27492pt}}}$}$\hfil\cr\hfil$\scriptstyle\Theta$\hfil\cr}}}}{{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\scriptscriptstyle\kern 0.15pt\scalebox{.8}[.65]{$\widehat{\phantom{\rule{8.0pt}{11.27492pt}}}$}$\hfil\cr\hfil$\scriptscriptstyle\Theta$\hfil\cr}}}}}{\mathchoice{{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\displaystyle\kern 0.15pt\scalebox{.8}[.65]{$\widehat{\phantom{\rule{8.0pt}{10.93333pt}}}$}$\hfil\cr\hfil$\displaystyle\Theta$\hfil\cr}}}}{{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\textstyle\kern 0.15pt\scalebox{.8}[.65]{$\widehat{\phantom{\rule{8.0pt}{10.93333pt}}}$}$\hfil\cr\hfil$\textstyle\Theta$\hfil\cr}}}}{{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\scriptstyle\kern 0.15pt\scalebox{.8}[.65]{$\widehat{\phantom{\rule{8.0pt}{10.93333pt}}}$}$\hfil\cr\hfil$\scriptstyle\Theta$\hfil\cr}}}}{{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\scriptscriptstyle\kern 0.15pt\scalebox{.8}[.65]{$\widehat{\phantom{\rule{8.0pt}{10.93333pt}}}$}$\hfil\cr\hfil$\scriptscriptstyle\Theta$\hfil\cr}}}}}{\widehat{\Theta}}{\widehat{\Theta}}
.

7.1
Interpretations

An
interpretation

\llbracket\operatorname{-\!-}\rrbracket
of
(\Sigma,\Gamma,\Theta)
over a KiCT

(\mathbf{C},\accentset{\rule{4.50697pt}{0.6pt}}{\mathbf{C}},\mathbf{C}^{?})
is specified as follows:

\displaystyle\llbracket 1\rrbracket\in\;

\displaystyle|\mathbf{C}|

\displaystyle\llbracket\mathsf{f}\rrbracket\in\;

\displaystyle\mathbf{C}(\llbracket 1\rrbracket,\llbracket n\rrbracket)\qquad(\mathsf{f}\in\Sigma_{n})

\displaystyle\llbracket\mathsf{u}\rrbracket\in\;

\displaystyle\accentset{\rule{4.50697pt}{0.6pt}}{\mathbf{C}}(\llbracket 1\rrbracket,\llbracket 1\rrbracket)\qquad(\mathsf{u}\in\Gamma)

\displaystyle\llbracket\mathsf{b}\rrbracket\in\;

\displaystyle\mathbf{C}^{?}(\llbracket 1\rrbracket,\llbracket 1\rrbracket)\qquad(\mathsf{b}\in\Theta)

where
\llbracket n\rrbracket
abbreviates the
n
-fold sum
\llbracket 1\rrbracket\oplus\ldots\oplus\llbracket 1\rrbracket
.
The latter immediately extends to

\widehat{\phantom{\rule{8.0pt}{10.93333pt}}}

\textstyle\Theta

:
\llbracket\{\,\}\rrbracket=1
,

\llbracket\{\mathsf{b}_{1},\ldots,\mathsf{b}_{n}\}\rrbracket=\mathsf{b}_{1}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}\ldots\mspace{};\mspace{.5}\mathsf{b}_{n}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}\bar{\mathsf{b}}_{n+1}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}\ldots\mspace{};\mspace{.5}\bar{\mathsf{b}}_{m}
,
assuming that
\Theta=\{\mathsf{b}_{1},\ldots,\mathsf{b}_{m}\}
.
Note that we interpret
n
-ary symbols over
\mathbf{C}(\llbracket 1\rrbracket,\llbracket n\rrbracket)=\mathbf{C}^{\mathsf{op}}(\llbracket 1\rrbracket^{n},\llbracket 1\rrbracket)
.
This equation seems to suggest that it could be more natural to use categories with products as models,
rather than categories with coproducts. Our present choice helps us to treat generic
KiCTs on the same footing with the free KiCT,
which is defined in terms of coproducts and not products.

Definition 30

(Free KiCT)
.

A
free KiCT
w.r.t.
(\Sigma,\Gamma,\Theta)
is a KiCT
(\mathfrak{F}_{\Sigma,\Gamma,\Theta},\overline{\mathfrak{F}_{\Sigma,\Gamma,\Theta}},{\mathfrak{F}^{?}_{\Sigma,\Gamma,\Theta}})
together with an interpretation of
(\Sigma,\Gamma,\Theta)
in
\mathfrak{F}_{\Sigma,\Gamma,\Theta}
, such that for any other interpretation of
(\Sigma,\Gamma,\Theta)
over a KiCT
(\mathbf{C},\accentset{\rule{4.50697pt}{0.6pt}}{\mathbf{C}},\mathbf{C}^{?})
, there is unique compatible functor from
\mathfrak{F}_{\Sigma,\Gamma,\Theta}

to
\mathbf{C}
. More formally, for any interpretation
\llbracket\operatorname{-\!-}\rrbracket
, there is unique KiCT-functor

\llbracket\operatorname{-\!-}\rrbracket_{\shortuparrow}\colon(\mathfrak{F}_{\Sigma,\Gamma,\Theta},\overline{\mathfrak{F}_{\Sigma,\Gamma,\Theta}},{\mathfrak{F}^{?}_{\Sigma,\Gamma,\Theta}})\to(\mathbf{C},\accentset{\rule{4.50697pt}{0.6pt}}{\mathbf{C}},\mathbf{C}^{?})

such that the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{F}_{\Sigma,\Gamma,\Theta}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\Sigma,\Gamma,\Theta)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\llbracket\operatorname{-\!-}\rrbracket_{\shortuparrow}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\llbracket\operatorname{-\!-}\rrbracket^{\mathfrak{F}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\llbracket\operatorname{-\!-}\rrbracket}

(9)

commutes.

In what follows, we characterize
\mathfrak{F}_{\Sigma,\Gamma,\Theta}
as a certain category
of rational trees, i.e. trees with finitely many distinct subtrees. An alternative,
equivalent formulation would be to view
\mathfrak{F}_{\Sigma,\Gamma,\Theta}
as a free
model of the (Lawvere) theory of KiCTs.

Like in the case of original Kozen’s completeness result
[
25
]
, a characterization
of the free model immediately entails completeness of the corresponding axiomatization over it.
Indeed, by generalities, a free KiCT is isomorphic to the free algebra of
terms, quotiented by the provable equality relation. Hence, if an equality holds
over the free model, it is provable.

7.2
A KiCT of Coalgebraic Resumptions

For any set
X
, define

TX=\mathcal{R}(\Gamma^{\Theta}\times X)
and

T_{\nu}X=\nu\gamma.\,T(X\oplus\Sigma\gamma)
,
in the category of sets
\mathbf{Set}
where
\mathcal{R}
is the monad from
19
.

A stepping stone for constructing
\mathfrak{F}_{\Sigma,\Gamma,\Theta}
is the
observation that
(\mathbf{Set}_{{\bm{\mathsf{T}}}_{\nu}},\mathbf{Set}_{{\bm{\mathsf{T}}}})
forms a KiC.
Indeed,
(\mathbf{Set}_{\mathcal{R}},\mathbf{Set}_{\mathcal{R}})
is a KiC and the monad
\mathcal{R}
is commutative,
hence symmetric monoidal. In
\mathbf{Set}_{\mathcal{R}}
,
\Gamma^{\Theta}
is a monoid
under the following operations:

\displaystyle\langle w_{1},\ldots,w_{n+1}\rangle\cdot\langle u_{1},\ldots,u_{m+1}\rangle=\begin{cases}\langle w_{1},\ldots,w_{n},u_{2},\ldots,u_{m+1}\rangle&\text{if }w_{n}=u_{1}\\
0&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

This produces the monad
{\bm{\mathsf{T}}}
, whose Kleisli category is a KiC by

Theorem 20
, analogously to
21
. Now,

(\mathbf{Set}_{{\bm{\mathsf{T}}}_{\nu}},\mathbf{Set}_{{\bm{\mathsf{T}}}})
is a KiC by
Theorem 23
. We will use the following
representation for generic elements of
T_{\nu}X
:

\displaystyle t=\sum_{i\in I}b_{i}.\,\mathsf{u}_{i}.\,t_{i}+\sum_{i\in J}b_{i}.\,\mathsf{f}_{i}(t_{i,1},\ldots,t_{i,n_{i}})+\sum_{i\in K}b_{i}.\,x_{i}

(10)

where
I
,
J
,
K
are mutually disjoint countable sets,
b_{i}
range over

\widehat{\phantom{\rule{8.0pt}{10.93333pt}}}
