,
\mathsf{f}\in\Sigma
,

3.

o(t)=o(s)
.

Then,
t=s
whenever
t\mathrel{\mathcal{B}}s
.

Proof Sketch.

It suffices to show that
\mathcal{B}
is a coalgebraic bisimulation. The claim is then a
consequence of strong extensionality of the final coalgebra
T_{\nu}X
. Let us spell
out what it means for
\mathcal{B}
to be a coalgebraic bisimulation. Given
t
and
t^{\prime}
, such
that
t\mathrel{\mathcal{B}}t^{\prime}
, and assuming representations

\displaystyle t=

\displaystyle\;\sum_{i\in I}g_{i}.\,\mathsf{f}_{i}(t_{i,1},\ldots,t_{i,n_{i}})+\sum_{i\in J}g_{i}.\,x_{i},

\displaystyle t^{\prime}=

\displaystyle\;\sum_{i\in I^{\prime}}g_{i}.\,\mathsf{f}_{i}(t_{i,1},\ldots,t_{i,n_{i}})+\sum_{i\in J^{\prime}}g_{i}.\,x_{i}

where the
g_{i}
range over
\Gamma^{\Theta}
, the sums
\sum_{i\in J}g_{i}.\,x_{i}

and
\sum_{i\in J^{\prime}}g_{i}.\,x_{i}
must be equal, and there must exist a set
K
and
surjections
e\colon K\to I
,
e^{\prime}\colon K\to I^{\prime}
, such that for every
k\in K
,

g_{e(k)}=g_{e^{\prime}(k)}
,
\mathsf{f}_{e(k)}=\mathsf{f}_{e^{\prime}(k)}
and
t_{e(k),1}\mathrel{\mathcal{B}}t_{e^{\prime}(k),1},\ldots,t_{e(k),m}\mathrel{\mathcal{B}}t_{e^{\prime}(k),m}
where
m
is the arity of
\mathsf{f}_{e(k)}
. This is indeed true
for
\mathcal{B}
. The reason for it is that
t
and
t^{\prime}
can be represented as

\displaystyle t=

\displaystyle\;\sum_{n\in{\rm Nature}}\sum_{i\in I,|g_{i}|=n}g_{i}.\,\mathsf{f}_{i}(t_{i,1},\ldots,t_{i,n_{i}})+\sum_{n\in{\rm Nature}}\sum_{i\in J,|g_{i}|=n}g_{i}.\,x_{i},

\displaystyle t^{\prime}=

\displaystyle\;\sum_{n\in{\rm Nature}}\sum_{i\in I^{\prime},|g_{i}|=n}g_{i}.\,\mathsf{f}_{i}(t_{i,1}^{\prime},\ldots,t_{i,n_{i}}^{\prime})+\sum_{n\in{\rm Nature}}\sum_{i\in J^{\prime},|g_{i}|=n}g_{i}.\,x_{i}

and we can derive the requisite properties for inner sums by induction on
n

from the assumptions.∎

7.3
Rational Trees

In what follows, we identify every
n\in{\rm Nature}
with the set
\{0,\ldots,n-1\}
,
and select binary coproducts in
\mathbf{Set}
so that
n\oplus m=\{0,\ldots,n-1\}\oplus\{0,\ldots,m-1\}=\{0,\ldots,n+m-1\}=n+m
.
The inclusion of
n
to
m\geq n
is then a coproduct injection, which we refer to
as
\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{n}^{m}
.

Definition 34

(Prefinite, Flat, (Non-)Guarded, Rational, Definable)
.

1.

The set of
prefinite
elements of
T_{\nu}X
is defined by induction:

t\in T_{\nu}X
of the form (
10
) is
prefinite
if the involved
sums contain finitely many distinct elements and all the
{t_{i},t_{i,j}\in T_{\nu}X}

are prefinite.

2.

A prefinite
t\in T_{\nu}X
of the form (
10
) is
flat
if
{t_{i},t_{i,j}\in X}
.

3.

An element
t\in T_{\nu}X
of the form (
10
) is
guarded
if
K=\emptyset
.

4.

An element
t\in T_{\nu}X
of the form (
10
) is
non-guarded
if
I\cup J=\emptyset
.

5.

An element
t\in T_{\nu}X
is
rational
if
\mathfrak{D}(t)
is finite and
t
depends on a finite subset of
\Sigma\cup\Gamma
.

A map
t\colon Y\to T_{\nu}X
is prefinite/flat/guarded/non-guarded if correspondingly
for every
x\in X
, every
t(x)
is prefinite/flat/guarded/non-guarded. Finally:

6.

A map
t\colon k\to T_{\nu}n
(with
k,n\in{\rm Nature}
) is
definable
if for some
m\geq k

there is flat guarded
s\colon m\to T_{\nu}m
and non-guarded
r\colon m\to T_{\nu}n
,
such that
t=\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{k}^{m}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}s^{\ast}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}r^{\sharp}
.

Using
32
, one can show

Lemma 35
.

Sum, composition and Kleene iteration of rational maps are again rational.

The following property is a form of Kleene theorem, originally stating the equivalence of regular
and recognizable languages
[
38
]
. In our setting it is proven with the help of
33
.

Proposition 36
.

Given
n,k\in{\rm Nature}
, a map
n\to T_{\nu}k
is rational iff it is definable.

Let
\mathfrak{F}=\mathfrak{F}_{\Sigma,\Gamma,\Theta}
be the (non-full) subcategory of

\mathbf{Set}_{{\bm{\mathsf{T}}}_{\nu}}
, identified as follows:

•

the objects of
\mathfrak{F}
are positive natural numbers,

•

the morphisms in
\mathfrak{F}(n,k)
are rational maps
f\colon n\to T_{\nu}k

(equivalently: (co)tuples
[t_{0},\ldots,t_{n-1}]
of rational elements of
T_{\nu}k
).

Let the wide subcategory of tame morphisms
\overline{\mathfrak{F}}
consist of such
tuples
[t_{0},\ldots,t_{n-1}]
that
{t_{i}\in Tk}
for all
i
, and let
\mathfrak{F}^{?}
consist of
those maps in
\overline{\mathfrak{F}}
that do not involve symbols from
\Gamma
.
This defines a KiCT essentially due to the closure properties from
35
.

Given an interpretation
\llbracket\operatorname{-\!-}\rrbracket\colon(\Sigma,\Gamma,\Theta)\to\mathbf{C}
, let us
extend it to flat elements first via

\displaystyle\hskip-1.99997pt\llbracket 0\rrbracket=0,\quad~\llbracket t+s\rrbracket=\llbracket t\rrbracket+\llbracket s\rrbracket,\quad~\llbracket\eta^{\ast}\rrbracket=\llbracket 1\rrbracket^{\ast},\quad~\llbracket t\mspace{};\mspace{.5}s^{\sharp}\rrbracket=\llbracket t\rrbracket\mspace{};\mspace{.5}\llbracket s\rrbracket,\quad~\llbracket k\rrbracket=\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{k}~(k\in n)

where
\eta^{\ast}
stands for a tuple of infinite sum
[\sum_{i\in{\rm Nature}}\{\}.0,\ldots,\sum_{i\in{\rm Nature}}\{\}.(n-1)]
,
and is the interpretation of
\eta^{\star}
in
\mathfrak{F}
. The clause for
\eta^{\ast}

in necessary to cater for infinite sums that can occur in prefinite elements. Such sums
can only contain finitely many distinct elements, and thus can be expressed
via finite sums and composition with
\eta^{\ast}
. Next, define
{\llbracket\operatorname{-\!-}\rrbracket_{\shortuparrow}\colon\mathfrak{F}\to\mathbf{C}}

•

on objects via
\llbracket n\rrbracket_{\shortuparrow}=\llbracket 1\rrbracket\oplus\ldots\oplus\llbracket 1\rrbracket
(
\llbracket 1\rrbracket
repeated
n
times),

•

on morphisms, via
\llbracket t\rrbracket_{\shortuparrow}=\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{n,m}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}\llbracket s\rrbracket^{\ast}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}\llbracket r\rrbracket
, where

t=\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{n,m}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}t^{\ast}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}r^{\sharp}
, for a guarded flat
s\colon m\to T_{\nu}m
, and a non-guarded
r\colon m\to T_{\nu}k
,
computed with
36
.

Theorem 37
.

\mathfrak{F}_{\Sigma,\Gamma,\Theta}
is a free KiCT over
\Sigma,\Gamma,\Theta
.

The following property is instrumental for proving this result:

Lemma 38
.

Let
(\mathbf{C},\accentset{\rule{4.50697pt}{0.6pt}}{\mathbf{C}})
and
(\mathbf{D},\accentset{\rule{4.50697pt}{0.6pt}}{\mathbf{D}})
be two KiCs, and let
F
be the following
map, acting on objects and on morphisms:
FX\in|\mathbf{D}|
for every
X\in|\mathbf{C}|
,

Fp\in\mathbf{D}(FX,FY)
for every
p\in\mathbf{C}(X,Y)
. Suppose that
F
preserves coproducts,
Fp\in\accentset{\rule{4.50697pt}{0.6pt}}{\mathbf{D}}(FX,FY)
for all
p\in\accentset{\rule{4.50697pt}{0.6pt}}{\mathbf{C}}(X,Y)
.

F
is a KiC-functor if the following further preservation properties hold

\displaystyle Fp^{\ast}=(Fp)^{\ast},\quad F(p\mspace{};\mspace{.5}\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{0}}})=Fp\mspace{};\mspace{.5}\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{0}}},\quad F(p\mspace{};\mspace{.5}\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{1}}})=Fp\mspace{};\mspace{.5}\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{1}}},\quad F(p\mspace{};\mspace{.5}[0,1])=Fp\mspace{};\mspace{.5}[0,1].

Let us briefly outline a potential application of
Theorem 37
to may-diverge
Kleene algebras, which we informally described in the introduction. Let us now define them
formally:

Definition 39

(May-Diverge Kleene Algebra)
.

A
may-diverge Kleene algebra
is an idempotent semiring
(S,\operatorname{}\linebreak 0,\operatorname{}\linebreak 1,\operatorname{}\linebreak+,\operatorname{}\linebreak\,\mspace{};\mspace{.5})

equipped with an iteration operator
(\operatorname{-\!-})^{\ast}\colon S\to S
satisfying the laws:

\displaystyle p^{\ast}=1+p\mspace{};\mspace{.5}p^{\ast}\qquad(p+q)^{\ast}=p^{\ast}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}(q\mspace{};\mspace{.5}p^{\ast})^{\ast}\qquad\vbox{$r\mspace{};\mspace{.5}p^{\ast}=q^{\ast}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}rr\mspace{};\mspace{.5}p=q\mspace{};\mspace{.5}r$}

Thus, may-diverge Kleene algebras are very close to KiCs of the form
(\mathbf{C},\mathbf{C})
with
|\mathbf{C}|=1
,
except that in our present treatment all KiCs come with binary coproducts as an
additional structure. We conjecture though that any may-diverge Kleene algebra,
viewed as a category, can be embedded to a KiC
(\mathbf{C},\mathbf{C})
with
|\mathbf{C}|=\{1,2,\ldots\}
.
Theorem 37

will then entail a characterization of the free may-diverge Kleene algebra on
\Gamma

as the full subcategory induced by the single object
1
of the Kleisli category of the monad
TX=\mathcal{R}(\Gamma^{\star}\times X)
. In other words, the free may-diverge Kleene algebra is carried (up-to-isomorphism)
by rational elements of
\mathcal{R}(\Gamma^{\star})
, similarly to that how the free Kleene
algebra is carried by rational elements of
{\mathcal{P}}(\Gamma^{\star})
.

8
Conclusions and Further Work

We developed a general and robust categorical notion of Kleene iteration –
KiC(T) (=Kleene-iteration category (with tests)) – inspired
by Kleene algebra (with tests) and its numerous cousins. We attested this
notion with various yardsticks: stability under the generalized
coalgebraic resumption monad transformer (hence under the exception transformer,
as its degenerate case), equivalence to the classical notion of Conway
iteration and to a suitably axiomatized theory of while-loops, but most remarkably, we
established an explicit description of the ensuing free model, as a category of certain
nondeterministic rational trees, playing the same role for our theory as the
algebra of regular events for Kleene algebra. However, in our case, the free model is
much more intricate and difficult to construct, as the iteration operator of it
is neither a least fixpoint nor a unique fixpoint. A salient feature of our notion, mirrored
in the structure of the free model, is that it can mediate between linear time and
branching time semantics via corresponding specified classes
of morphisms.

Given the abstract nature of our results, we expect them be be reusable for varying
and enriching the core notion of Kleene iteration with other features. For example,
our underlying notion of nondeterminism is that of idempotent
grove category. General grove categories are a natural base for probabilistic or graded semantics,
and we expect that most of our results, including completeness can be adapted to this case.
Yet more generally, a relevant ingredient of our construction is monad
\mathcal{R}
, currently capturing the effect
of nondeterminism, but which can potentially be varied to obtain other flavors
of linear behavior.

An important open problem that remains for future work is that of defining KiCTs without
coproducts, potentially providing a bridge to relevant algebraic structures as single-object
categories. Now that the free KiCT with coproduct is identified, the free KiCT
without coproducts is expected to be complete over the same model. Identifying such a notion
is hard, because it would simultaneously encompass independent axiomatizations of iterative behavior,
e.g. branching time and linear time. As of now, such axiomatizations
are built on hard-to-reconcile approaches to iteration as either a least or
a unique fixpoint.

Appendix: Selected Proof Details

I.
Proof of
7

Suppose that
d
is linear. Then

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathsf{\underline{if}}}\mathbin{}d\operatorname{\mathsf{\underline{then}}}\mathbin{}p\operatorname{\mathsf{\underline{else}}}\mathbin{\,}q

\displaystyle\;=d\mspace{};\mspace{.5}[q,p]

\displaystyle\;=d\mspace{};\mspace{.5}([q,0]+[0,p])

\displaystyle\;=d\mspace{};\mspace{.5}[q,0]+d\mspace{};\mspace{.5}[0,p]

\displaystyle\;=d\mspace{};\mspace{.5}[1,0]\mspace{};\mspace{.5}q+d\mspace{};\mspace{.5}[0,1]\mspace{};\mspace{.5}p

\displaystyle\;=\operatorname{\mathsf{if}}\mathbin{}d?\operatorname{\mathsf{then}}\mathbin{}p\operatorname{\mathsf{else}}\mathbin{}q.

II.
Proof of
8

The necessity is obvious. Let us show sufficiency. For every
b\in\mathbf{C}^{?}(X)
,
let us fix some choice of
\bar{b}\in\mathbf{C}^{?}(X)
, for which the pair
(b,\bar{b})
satisfies contradiction and excluded middle,
and show that
\mathbf{C}^{?}(X)
forms a Boolean algebra, i.e.
\mathbf{C}^{?}(X)

is a complemented distributive lattice. Complementation precisely amounts to the
assumed identities, and we are left to show the laws of distributive lattices.
Since complements are uniquely defined in Boolean algebras, it will follow that

\bar{b}
is uniquely determined by
b
. Of course, this is not used in the subsequent proof.

It follows
by definition that
(\mathbf{C}^{?}(X),0,+)
and
(\mathbf{C}^{?}(X),1,\mspace{};\mspace{.5})
are monoids.
Showing that they are idempotent and commutative (hence, are semilattices) amounts to showing that

b\mspace{};\mspace{.5}b=b
and
b\mspace{};\mspace{.5}c=c\mspace{};\mspace{.5}b
for all
b,c\in\mathbf{C}^{?}(X)
. The first identity is shown
as follows, using linearity and the assumed identities:

\displaystyle b=

\displaystyle\;b\mspace{};\mspace{.5}1

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;b\mspace{};\mspace{.5}(b+\bar{b})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;b\mspace{};\mspace{.5}b+b\mspace{};\mspace{.5}\bar{b}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;b\mspace{};\mspace{.5}b+0

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;b\mspace{};\mspace{.5}b.

For the second one, note that
1=b+\bar{b}=b+b+\bar{b}=b+1
,
and then

\displaystyle b\mspace{};\mspace{.5}c=

\displaystyle\;b\mspace{};\mspace{.5}c\mspace{};\mspace{.5}1

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;b\mspace{};\mspace{.5}c\mspace{};\mspace{.5}(b+1)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;b\mspace{};\mspace{.5}c\mspace{};\mspace{.5}b+b\mspace{};\mspace{.5}c

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;b\mspace{};\mspace{.5}c\mspace{};\mspace{.5}b+b\mspace{};\mspace{.5}c\mspace{};\mspace{.5}b\mspace{};\mspace{.5}c

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;b\mspace{};\mspace{.5}c\mspace{};\mspace{.5}b\mspace{};\mspace{.5}(1+c)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;b\mspace{};\mspace{.5}c\mspace{};\mspace{.5}b,

where we used the instance of idempotence
b\mspace{};\mspace{.5}c=b\mspace{};\mspace{.5}c\mspace{};\mspace{.5}b\mspace{};\mspace{.5}c
that we just
established.
Analogously,
c\mspace{};\mspace{.5}b=b\mspace{};\mspace{.5}c\mspace{};\mspace{.5}b
, and hence
b\mspace{};\mspace{.5}c=c\mspace{};\mspace{.5}b
.

Finally,
distributivity amounts to
(a+b)\mspace{};\mspace{.5}c=a\mspace{};\mspace{.5}c+b\mspace{};\mspace{.5}c
and

a+b\mspace{};\mspace{.5}c=(a+b)\mspace{};\mspace{.5}(a+c)
for all
a,b,c\in\mathbf{C}^{?}(X)
.
The first identity is an axiom of idempotent grove categories. The second one is obtained as
follows:

\displaystyle(a+b)\mspace{};\mspace{.5}(a+c)=

\displaystyle\;a\mspace{};\mspace{.5}a+a\mspace{};\mspace{.5}c+b\mspace{};\mspace{.5}a+b\mspace{};\mspace{.5}c\qquad

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;a+a\mspace{};\mspace{.5}c+a\mspace{};\mspace{.5}b+b\mspace{};\mspace{.5}c

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;a\mspace{};\mspace{.5}(1+c+b)+b\mspace{};\mspace{.5}c

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;a\mspace{};\mspace{.5}1+b\mspace{};\mspace{.5}c

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;a+b\mspace{};\mspace{.5}c

∎

III.
Proof of
10

Negation is defined as follows:

\displaystyle\bar{0}=

\displaystyle\;1,

\displaystyle\overline{[\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{0}}},0]}=

\displaystyle\;[0,\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{1}}}],

\displaystyle\overline{b+c}=

\displaystyle\;\bar{b}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}\bar{c},

\displaystyle\bar{1}=

\displaystyle\;0,

\displaystyle\overline{[0,\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{1}}}]}=

\displaystyle\;[\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{0}}},0],

\displaystyle\overline{b\mspace{};\mspace{.5}c}=

\displaystyle\;\bar{b}+\bar{c}.

For every
X
, let
\mathbf{C}^{?}(X)
be the smallest subset of
\mathbf{C}(X,X)
that
contains
0
,
1
,
[\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{0}}},0]
and
[0,\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{1}}}]
, and closed under
+
and
\!\mspace{};\mspace{.5}
.
By
8
, we need to show that
every
b\in\mathbf{C}^{?}(X)
is linear and satisfies
b\mspace{};\mspace{.5}\bar{b}=0
,

b+\bar{b}=1
, which we do by induction. Let us strengthen the induction invariant
by also adding
\bar{b}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}b=0
. Note that the above equations do not uniquely
define complement, e.g.
\overline{[\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{0}}},0]}=\;[0,\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{1}}}]
refers to a
particular decomposition of
X
as
X_{1}\oplus X_{2}
, while another decomposition
could theoretically produce a different result. Thus, more precisely, we use the
fact that every element of
\mathbf{C}^{?}(X)
has a representation in the free algebra of
terms over
0
,
1
,
[\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{0}}},0]
,
[0,\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{1}}}]
,
+
and
\mspace{};\mspace{.5}
, and
we run induction over this representation.

Induction Base.
If
b=0
or
b=1
, the verification is trivial.
Consider
b=[\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{0}}},0]
. Then

\displaystyle b\mspace{};\mspace{.5}0=

\displaystyle\;[\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{0}}},0]\mspace{};\mspace{.5}0

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;[\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{0}}}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}0,0]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;0,

\displaystyle b\mspace{};\mspace{.5}(p+q)=

\displaystyle\;[\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{0}}},0]\mspace{};\mspace{.5}(p+q)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;[\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{0}}}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}p+\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{0}}}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}q,0]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;[\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{0}}}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}p,0]+[\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{0}}}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}q,0]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;b\mspace{};\mspace{.5}p+b\mspace{};\mspace{.5}q,

for any suitably
typed
p
and
q
; analogously
\bar{b}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}0=0
,
\bar{b}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}(p+q)=\bar{b}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}p+\bar{b}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}q
. Moreover,

\displaystyle b\mspace{};\mspace{.5}\bar{b}=

\displaystyle\;[\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{0}}},0]\mspace{};\mspace{.5}[0,\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{1}}}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;[\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{0}}}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}[0,\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{1}}}],0\mspace{};\mspace{.5}[0,\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{1}}}]]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;0,

\displaystyle b+\bar{b}=

\displaystyle\;[\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{0}}},0]+[0,\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{1}}}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;[\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{0}}}+0,0+\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{1}}}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;1.

The identity
\bar{b}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}b=0
is shown analogously to
b\mspace{};\mspace{.5}\bar{b}=0
.

Induction Step.

Suppose that the induction hypothesis holds for some
{b,c\in\mathbf{C}^{?}(X)}

and show that
b\mspace{};\mspace{.5}c
,
b+c
are both linear and satisfy equations:

\displaystyle b\mspace{};\mspace{.5}c\mspace{};\mspace{.5}(\bar{b}+\bar{c})=

\displaystyle\;0,

\displaystyle(\bar{b}+\bar{c})\mspace{};\mspace{.5}b\mspace{};\mspace{.5}c=

\displaystyle\;0,

\displaystyle b\mspace{};\mspace{.5}c+(\bar{b}+\bar{c})=

\displaystyle\;1,

(11)

\displaystyle(b+c)\mspace{};\mspace{.5}\bar{b}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}\bar{c}=

\displaystyle\;0,

\displaystyle\bar{b}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}\bar{c}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}(b+c)=

\displaystyle\;0,

\displaystyle(b+c)+\bar{b}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}\bar{c}=

\displaystyle\;1.

(12)

Linearity of
(b\mspace{};\mspace{.5}c)
:

\displaystyle b\mspace{};\mspace{.5}c\mspace{};\mspace{.5}0

\displaystyle\;=b\mspace{};\mspace{.5}0

\displaystyle\;=0,

\displaystyle b\mspace{};\mspace{.5}c\mspace{};\mspace{.5}(p+q)

\displaystyle\;=b\mspace{};\mspace{.5}(b\mspace{};\mspace{.5}p+c\mspace{};\mspace{.5}q)

\displaystyle\;=b\mspace{};\mspace{.5}c\mspace{};\mspace{.5}p+b\mspace{};\mspace{.5}b\mspace{};\mspace{.5}q.

Linearity of
(b+c)
:

\displaystyle(b+c)\mspace{};\mspace{.5}0

\displaystyle\;=b\mspace{};\mspace{.5}0+c\mspace{};\mspace{.5}0

\displaystyle\;=0,

\displaystyle(b+c)\mspace{};\mspace{.5}(p+q)

\displaystyle\;=b\mspace{};\mspace{.5}(p+q)+c\mspace{};\mspace{.5}(p+q)

\displaystyle\;=(b\mspace{};\mspace{.5}p+b\mspace{};\mspace{.5}q)+(c\mspace{};\mspace{.5}p+c\mspace{};\mspace{.5}q)

\displaystyle\;=(b+c)\mspace{};\mspace{.5}p+(b+c)\mspace{};\mspace{.5}q.

Finally, we prove two equations from the list (
11
)–(
12
) –
the rest is analogous. Note that
c+1=c+c+\bar{c}=c+\bar{c}=1
,

\displaystyle b\mspace{};\mspace{.5}c\mspace{};\mspace{.5}(\bar{b}+\bar{c})

\displaystyle\;=b\mspace{};\mspace{.5}c\mspace{};\mspace{.5}\bar{b}+b\mspace{};\mspace{.5}c\mspace{};\mspace{.5}\bar{c}

\displaystyle\;=b\mspace{};\mspace{.5}c\mspace{};\mspace{.5}\bar{b}+b\mspace{};\mspace{.5}0

\displaystyle\;=b\mspace{};\mspace{.5}c\mspace{};\mspace{.5}\bar{b}

\displaystyle\;=b\mspace{};\mspace{.5}c\mspace{};\mspace{.5}\bar{b}+b\mspace{};\mspace{.5}\bar{b}

\displaystyle\;=b\mspace{};\mspace{.5}(c+1)\mspace{};\mspace{.5}\bar{b}

\displaystyle\;=b\mspace{};\mspace{.5}1\mspace{};\mspace{.5}\bar{b}

\displaystyle\;=0.

\displaystyle b\mspace{};\mspace{.5}c+(\bar{b}+\bar{c})

\displaystyle\;=b\mspace{};\mspace{.5}c+\bar{b}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}1+1\mspace{};\mspace{.5}\bar{c}

\displaystyle\;=b\mspace{};\mspace{.5}c+\bar{b}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}(c+\bar{c})+(b+\bar{b})\mspace{};\mspace{.5}\bar{c}

\displaystyle\;=b\mspace{};\mspace{.5}c+\bar{b}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}c+\bar{b}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}\bar{c}+b\mspace{};\mspace{.5}\bar{c}+\bar{b}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}\bar{c}

\displaystyle\;=b\mspace{};\mspace{.5}(c+\bar{c})+\bar{b}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}(c+\bar{c})

\displaystyle\;=(b+\bar{b})\mspace{};\mspace{.5}(c+\bar{c})

\displaystyle\;=1\mspace{};\mspace{.5}1

\displaystyle\;=1.

∎

IV.
Proof of
11

Clause 1 is shown as follows:

\displaystyle(\mathinner{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\scalebox{1.35}{$\diamond$}}}b)?

\displaystyle\;=(\bar{b}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{0}}}+b\mspace{};\mspace{.5}\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{1}}})\mspace{};\mspace{.5}[0,1]

\displaystyle\;=b.

from the image

as desired. The dual equation

Finally, Clause 3 is shown as follows:

\displaystyle(e\mspace{};\mspace{.5}[d,\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{1}}}])?

VI.
Proof of
Theorem 20

Note that the assumptions imply that
T
is a KiC-functor, in particular,
T0=0
and
T(p+q)=Tp+Tq
,
by
41
. The laws of grove categories are easy to check. Indeed, the equations (
3
)
interpreted in
\mathbf{C}_{{\bm{\mathsf{T}}}}
turn into

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad 0\mspace{};\mspace{.5}p^{\sharp}=

\displaystyle\;0,

\displaystyle(q+r)\mspace{};\mspace{.5}p^{\sharp}=

\displaystyle\;q\mspace{};\mspace{.5}p^{\sharp}+r\mspace{};\mspace{.5}p^{\sharp},

and clearly follow from assumptions. Linearity of left injections in
\mathbf{C}_{{\bm{\mathsf{T}}}}
reads as

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{0}}}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}\eta\mspace{};\mspace{.5}0^{\sharp}=

\displaystyle\;0,

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{0}}}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}\eta\mspace{};\mspace{.5}(q+r)^{\sharp}=

\displaystyle\;\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{0}}}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}\eta\mspace{};\mspace{.5}q^{\sharp}+\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{0}}}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}\eta\mspace{};\mspace{.5}r^{\sharp},

and follows from the assumption that
\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{0}}}
is linear in
\mathbf{C}
– analogously for
\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{1}}}
.

Let us check that the morphisms from
\accentset{\rule{4.50697pt}{0.6pt}}{\mathbf{C}}_{\bm{\mathsf{T}}}
are all linear. To that end,
take some
p
from
\accentset{\rule{4.50697pt}{0.6pt}}{\mathbf{C}}_{\bm{\mathsf{T}}}
, and show

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad p\mspace{};\mspace{.5}0^{\sharp}=

\displaystyle\;0,

\displaystyle p\mspace{};\mspace{.5}(q+r)^{\sharp}=

\displaystyle\;p\mspace{};\mspace{.5}q^{\sharp}+p\mspace{};\mspace{.5}r^{\sharp}

Using the assumptions,
p\mspace{};\mspace{.5}0^{\sharp}=p\mspace{};\mspace{.5}T0\mspace{};\mspace{.5}\mu=p\mspace{};\mspace{.5}0\mspace{};\mspace{.5}\mu=0\mspace{};\mspace{.5}\mu=0
where

\mu=1^{\sharp}
is multiplication of
{\bm{\mathsf{T}}}
. The second equation is shown analogously.

Finally, we proceed to verify the laws of iteration. To that end, we record some
auxiliary properties:

\displaystyle(\eta\mspace{};\mspace{.5}(p^{\sharp})^{\ast})^{\sharp}

\displaystyle=(p^{\sharp})^{\ast},

(14)

\displaystyle\eta\mspace{};\mspace{.5}(p^{\sharp}+q^{\sharp})

\displaystyle=p+q.

(15)

For the first identity, note that

\displaystyle Tp^{\sharp}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}\mu=TTp\mspace{};\mspace{.5}T\mu\mspace{};\mspace{.5}\mu

\displaystyle=TTp\mspace{};\mspace{.5}\mu\mspace{};\mspace{.5}\mu=\mu\mspace{};\mspace{.5}Tp\mspace{};\mspace{.5}\mu=\mu\mspace{};\mspace{.5}p^{\sharp}.

By assumption,
\mu=1^{\sharp}
is tame, hence, by
\ast
-Uni
,

\displaystyle(Tp^{\sharp})^{\ast}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}\mu

\displaystyle=\mu\mspace{};\mspace{.5}(p^{\sharp})^{\ast}.

We then have

\displaystyle(\eta\mspace{};\mspace{.5}(p^{\sharp})^{\ast})^{\sharp}

\displaystyle=T\eta\mspace{};\mspace{.5}T(p^{\sharp})^{\ast}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}\mu

\displaystyle=T\eta\mspace{};\mspace{.5}(Tp^{\sharp})^{\ast}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}\mu

\displaystyle=T\eta\mspace{};\mspace{.5}\mu\mspace{};\mspace{.5}(p^{\sharp})^{\ast}

\displaystyle=(\eta\mspace{};\mspace{.5}(p^{\sharp})^{\ast})^{\sharp}.

The second identity is shown as follows:

\displaystyle\eta\mspace{};\mspace{.5}(p^{\sharp}+q^{\sharp})

\displaystyle=\eta\mspace{};\mspace{.5}(Tp\mspace{};\mspace{.5}\mu+Tq\mspace{};\mspace{.5}\mu)

\displaystyle=\eta\mspace{};\mspace{.5}(Tp+Tq)\mspace{};\mspace{.5}\mu

\displaystyle=\eta\mspace{};\mspace{.5}T(p+q)\mspace{};\mspace{.5}\mu

\displaystyle=(p+q)\mspace{};\mspace{.5}\eta\mspace{};\mspace{.5}\mu

\displaystyle=p+q.

Now, the laws of KiCs are shown as follows:

\ast
-Fix
: We need to show
\eta\mspace{};\mspace{.5}(p^{\sharp})^{\ast}=\eta+p\mspace{};\mspace{.5}(\eta\mspace{};\mspace{.5}(p^{\sharp})^{\ast})^{\sharp}
. Indeed,

\displaystyle\eta\mspace{};\mspace{.5}(p^{\sharp})^{\ast}

\displaystyle\;=\eta\mspace{};\mspace{.5}(1+p^{\sharp}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}(p^{\sharp})^{\ast})

\displaystyle\;=\eta\mspace{};\mspace{.5}(1+p^{\sharp}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}(\eta\mspace{};\mspace{.5}(p^{\sharp})^{\ast})^{\sharp})

/​​/ (
14
)

\displaystyle\;=\eta\mspace{};\mspace{.5}(\eta^{\sharp}+(p\mspace{};\mspace{.5}(\eta\mspace{};\mspace{.5}(p^{\sharp})^{\ast})^{\sharp})^{\sharp})

\displaystyle\;=\eta+p\mspace{};\mspace{.5}(\eta\mspace{};\mspace{.5}(p^{\sharp})^{\ast})^{\sharp}.

/​​/ (
15
)

\ast
-Sum
: We need to show
\eta\mspace{};\mspace{.5}((p+q)^{\sharp})^{\ast}=\eta\mspace{};\mspace{.5}(p^{\sharp})^{\ast}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}(\eta\mspace{};\mspace{.5}(q\mspace{};\mspace{.5}(\eta\mspace{};\mspace{.5}(p^{\sharp})^{\ast})^{\sharp})^{\ast})^{\sharp}
.
This is essentially obtained from
\ast
-Sum
for
(\mathbf{C},\accentset{\rule{4.50697pt}{0.6pt}}{\mathbf{C}})
by repeated use of (
15
):

\displaystyle\eta\mspace{};\mspace{.5}((p+q)^{\sharp})^{\ast}

\displaystyle\;=\eta\mspace{};\mspace{.5}(p^{\sharp}+q^{\sharp})^{\ast}

\displaystyle\;=\eta\mspace{};\mspace{.5}(p^{\sharp})^{\ast}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}(q^{\sharp}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}(p^{\sharp})^{\ast})^{\ast}

\displaystyle\;=\eta\mspace{};\mspace{.5}(p^{\sharp})^{\ast}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}(q^{\sharp}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}(\eta\mspace{};\mspace{.5}(p^{\sharp})^{\ast})^{\sharp})^{\ast}

\displaystyle\;=\eta\mspace{};\mspace{.5}(p^{\sharp})^{\ast}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}((q\mspace{};\mspace{.5}(\eta\mspace{};\mspace{.5}(p^{\sharp})^{\ast})^{\sharp})^{\sharp})^{\ast}

\displaystyle\;=\eta\mspace{};\mspace{.5}(p^{\sharp})^{\ast}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}(\eta\mspace{};\mspace{.5}(q\mspace{};\mspace{.5}(\eta\mspace{};\mspace{.5}(p^{\sharp})^{\ast})^{\sharp})^{\ast})^{\sharp}.

\ast
-Uni
: Suppose that
u\mspace{};\mspace{.5}p^{\sharp}=q\mspace{};\mspace{.5}u^{\sharp}
for some
tame
u
. We need to show
u\mspace{};\mspace{.5}(\eta\mspace{};\mspace{.5}(p^{\sharp})^{\ast})^{\sharp}=\eta\mspace{};\mspace{.5}(q^{\sharp})^{\ast}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}u^{\sharp}
.
By applying Kleisli lifting to both sides of the assumption, we obtain

u^{\sharp}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}p^{\sharp}=q^{\sharp}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}u^{\sharp}
, which by
\ast
-Uni

yields
u^{\sharp}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}(p^{\sharp})^{\ast}=(q^{\sharp})^{\ast}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}u^{\sharp}
. Then

\displaystyle u\mspace{};\mspace{.5}(\eta\mspace{};\mspace{.5}(p^{\sharp})^{\ast})^{\sharp}

\displaystyle\;=u\mspace{};\mspace{.5}(p^{\sharp})^{\ast}

/​​/ (
15
)

\displaystyle\;=\eta\mspace{};\mspace{.5}u^{\sharp}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}(p^{\sharp})^{\ast}

\displaystyle\;=\eta\mspace{};\mspace{.5}(q^{\sharp})^{\ast}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}u^{\sharp},

and we are done.
∎

VII.
Proof of
Theorem 26

The present construction is an improvement of a previous one
[
16
, Theorem 18]
, which we partially reuse here.
Two important changes are responsible for redesigning the previous argument:
here we redefine
DW-And
, and make it an axiom (instead of a rule, as before),
and we restrict the use of
DW-Uni
, by making more conservative assumptions
about the subcategory
\mathbf{D}
.

The requisite mutual translations are as follows:

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathsf{\underline{while}}}\mathbin{\,}d\mathbin{}\operatorname{\mathsf{\underline{do}}}\mathbin{\,}p=

\displaystyle\;(\operatorname{\mathsf{\underline{if}}}\mathbin{}d\operatorname{\mathsf{\underline{then}}}\mathbin{}p\mspace{};\mspace{.5}\operatorname{\mathsf{t\kern-0.45ptt}}\operatorname{\mathsf{\underline{else}}}\mathbin{\,}\operatorname{\mathsf{f\kern-0.78ptf}})^{\dagger},

(16)

\displaystyle p^{\dagger}=

\displaystyle\;\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{1}}}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}(\operatorname{\mathsf{\underline{while}}}\mathbin{\,}(\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{0}}}\oplus\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{1}}})\mathbin{}\operatorname{\mathsf{\underline{do}}}\mathbin{\,}[\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{0}}},p])\mspace{};\mspace{.5}[1,\delta]

(17)

where
\delta=\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{1}}}^{\dagger}
.

First, we show that the indicated translations are mutually inverse.

(i)
(\operatorname{-\!-})^{\dagger}\to\underline{\operatorname{\mathsf{while}}}\to(\operatorname{-\!-})^{\dagger}
: We need to show
that

\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{1}}}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}(\operatorname{\mathsf{\underline{if}}}\mathbin{}(\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{0}}}\oplus\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{1}}})\operatorname{\mathsf{\underline{then}}}\mathbin{}[\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{0}}},p]\mspace{};\mspace{.5}\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{1}}}\operatorname{\mathsf{\underline{else}}}\mathbin{\,}\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{0}}})^{\dagger}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}[1,\delta]=p^{\dagger}.

Indeed,

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{1}}}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}(\operatorname{\mathsf{\underline{if}}}\mathbin{}

\displaystyle(\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{0}}}\oplus\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{1}}})\operatorname{\mathsf{\underline{then}}}\mathbin{}[\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{0}}},p]\mspace{};\mspace{.5}\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{1}}}\operatorname{\mathsf{\underline{else}}}\mathbin{\,}\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{0}}})^{\dagger}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}[1,\delta]

\displaystyle\;=\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{1}}}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}((\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{0}}}\oplus\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{1}}})\mspace{};\mspace{.5}[\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{0}}},[\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{0}}},p]\mspace{};\mspace{.5}\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{1}}}])^{\dagger}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}[1,\delta]

\displaystyle\;=\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{1}}}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}(\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{0}}}\oplus p)^{\dagger}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}[1,\delta]

\displaystyle\;=\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{1}}}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}(1\oplus p)^{\dagger}

/​​/
Naturality

\displaystyle\;=\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{1}}}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}([\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{0}}},\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{1}}}]\mspace{};\mspace{.5}[\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{0}}},p\mspace{};\mspace{.5}\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{1}}}])^{\dagger}

\displaystyle\;=\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{1}}}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}[\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{0}}},\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{1}}}]\mspace{};\mspace{.5}[1,(p\mspace{};\mspace{.5}\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{1}}}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}[\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{0}}},[\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{0}}},\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{1}}}]])^{\dagger}]

/​​/
Dinaturality

\displaystyle\;=p^{\dagger}.

(ii)
\underline{\operatorname{\mathsf{while}}}\to(\operatorname{-\!-})^{\dagger}\to\underline{\operatorname{\mathsf{while}}}
: We need to show that

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{1}}}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}(\operatorname{\mathsf{\underline{while}}}\mathbin{\,}(\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{0}}}\oplus\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{1}}})\mathbin{}\operatorname{\mathsf{\underline{do}}}\mathbin{\,}[\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{0}}},\operatorname{\mathsf{\underline{if}}}\mathbin{}d\operatorname{\mathsf{\underline{then}}}\mathbin{}p\mspace{};\mspace{.5}\operatorname{\mathsf{t\kern-0.45ptt}}\operatorname{\mathsf{\underline{else}}}\mathbin{\,}\operatorname{\mathsf{f\kern-0.78ptf}}])\mspace{};\mspace{.5}[1,\delta]=\operatorname{\mathsf{\underline{while}}}\mathbin{\,}d\mathbin{}\operatorname{\mathsf{\underline{do}}}\mathbin{\,}p.

(18)

Observe that

\displaystyle[\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{0}}},\operatorname{\mathsf{\underline{if}}}\mathbin{}d\operatorname{\mathsf{\underline{then}}}\mathbin{}p\mspace{};\mspace{.5}

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathsf{t\kern-0.45ptt}}\operatorname{\mathsf{\underline{else}}}\mathbin{\,}\operatorname{\mathsf{f\kern-0.78ptf}}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;[\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{0}}},d]\mspace{};\mspace{.5}(1\oplus p)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;[\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{00}}},d\mspace{};\mspace{.5}(\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{0}}}\oplus\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{1}}})]\mspace{};\mspace{.5}[1\oplus p,1\oplus p]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\operatorname{\mathsf{\underline{if}}}\mathbin{}[\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{00}}},d\mspace{};\mspace{.5}(\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{0}}}\oplus\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{1}}})]\operatorname{\mathsf{\underline{then}}}\mathbin{}(1\oplus p)\operatorname{\mathsf{\underline{else}}}\mathbin{\,}(1\oplus p),

and

\displaystyle(\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{0}}}\oplus\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{1}}})\mathrel{\texttt{\&\kern-0.5pt\&}}(

\displaystyle[\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{00}}},d\mspace{};\mspace{.5}(\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{0}}}\oplus\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{1}}})]\mathrel{\texttt{|\kern-2.95pt|}}\operatorname{\mathsf{t\kern-0.45ptt}})

\displaystyle\;(\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{0}}}\oplus\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{1}}})\mspace{};\mspace{.5}[\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{0}}},[\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{01}}},d\mspace{};\mspace{.5}\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{1}}}]]

\displaystyle\;\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{0}}}\oplus d,

Hence, by

,

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathsf{\underline{while}}}\mathbin{\,}(\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{0}}}\oplus

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{1}}})\mathbin{}\operatorname{\mathsf{\underline{do}}}\mathbin{\,}[\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{0}}},\operatorname{\mathsf{\underline{if}}}\mathbin{}d\operatorname{\mathsf{\underline{then}}}\mathbin{}p\mspace{};\mspace{.5}\operatorname{\mathsf{t\kern-0.45ptt}}\operatorname{\mathsf{\underline{else}}}\mathbin{\,}\operatorname{\mathsf{f\kern-0.78ptf}}]=\operatorname{\mathsf{\underline{while}}}\mathbin{\,}(\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{0}}}\oplus d)\mathbin{}\operatorname{\mathsf{\underline{do}}}\mathbin{\,}(1\oplus p).

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{1}}}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}\operatorname{\mathsf{\underline{while}}}\mathbin{\,}e\mathbin{}\operatorname{\mathsf{\underline{do}}}\mathbin{\,}(1\oplus p)=

\displaystyle\;\operatorname{\mathsf{\underline{while}}}\mathbin{\,}d\mathbin{}\operatorname{\mathsf{\underline{do}}}\mathbin{\,}p\mspace{};\mspace{.5}\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{1}}}

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{1}}}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}\operatorname{\mathsf{\underline{while}}}\mathbin{\,}(\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{0}}}\oplus\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{0}}})\mathbin{}\operatorname{\mathsf{\underline{do}}}\mathbin{\,}((1\oplus p)\mspace{};\mspace{.5}\operatorname{\mathsf{\underline{while}}}\mathbin{\,}e\mathbin{}\operatorname{\mathsf{\underline{do}}}\mathbin{\,}(1\oplus p))=

\displaystyle\;\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{0}}}

The latter is straightforward by

. For the former, observe that

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{1}}}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}e=

\displaystyle\;\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{1}}}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}[\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{00}}},d\mspace{};\mspace{.5}(\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{1}}}\oplus\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{1}}})]=\operatorname{\mathsf{\underline{if}}}\mathbin{}d\operatorname{\mathsf{\underline{then}}}\mathbin{}\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{1}}}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}\operatorname{\mathsf{t\kern-0.45ptt}}\operatorname{\mathsf{\underline{else}}}\mathbin{\,}\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{1}}}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}\operatorname{\mathsf{f\kern-0.78ptf}},

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{1}}}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}(1\oplus p)=

\displaystyle\;p\mspace{};\mspace{.5}\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{1}}},

and use

(iii) We need to check that the laws of uniform Conway iteration follow from

\displaystyle=\;

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{1}}}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}[q\mspace{};\mspace{.5}\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{00}}},p\mspace{};\mspace{.5}(\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{01}}}\oplus\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{1}}})]^{\dagger\dagger}

/​​/

\displaystyle=\;

/​​/

VIII.

Lemma 40
.

\mathbf{C}
,
\mathbf{D}
and

Then the following identities are derivable:

\displaystyle\bar{b}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}\operatorname{\mathsf{while}}\mathbin{}(b\land c)\operatorname{\mathsf{do}}\mathbin{}p=

\displaystyle\;\bar{b}

(20)

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathsf{while}}\mathbin{}(b\lor c)\operatorname{\mathsf{do}}\mathbin{}p=

\displaystyle\;(\operatorname{\mathsf{while}}\mathbin{}(b\lor c)\operatorname{\mathsf{do}}\mathbin{}p)\mspace{};\mspace{.5}\bar{b}

(21)

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathsf{while}}\mathbin{}(b\land c)\operatorname{\mathsf{do}}\mathbin{}p=

\displaystyle\;\operatorname{\mathsf{while}}\mathbin{}(b\land c)\operatorname{\mathsf{do}}\mathbin{}(b\mspace{};\mspace{.5}p)

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathsf{while}}\mathbin{}b\operatorname{\mathsf{do}}\mathbin{}(\operatorname{\mathsf{if}}\mathbin{}c\operatorname{\mathsf{then}}\mathbin{}p\operatorname{\mathsf{else}}\mathbin{}q)=

\displaystyle(\operatorname{\mathsf{while}}\mathbin{}(b\,\land c)\operatorname{\mathsf{do}}\mathbin{}p)\mspace{};\mspace{.5}\,

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathsf{while}}\mathbin{}b\operatorname{\mathsf{do}}\mathbin{}(q\mspace{};\mspace{.5}\operatorname{\mathsf{while}}\mathbin{}(b\land c)\operatorname{\mathsf{do}}\mathbin{}p)

where the if-then-else operator is defined as in (

).

Proof.

(

) is shown directly:

\displaystyle\bar{b}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}\operatorname{\mathsf{while}}\mathbin{}

\displaystyle(b\land c)\operatorname{\mathsf{do}}\mathbin{}p

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\bar{b}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}\operatorname{\mathsf{if}}\mathbin{}(b\land c)\operatorname{\mathsf{then}}\mathbin{}p\mspace{};\mspace{.5}(\operatorname{\mathsf{while}}\mathbin{}(b\land c)\operatorname{\mathsf{do}}\mathbin{}p)\operatorname{\mathsf{else}}\mathbin{}1

/​​/

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\bar{b}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}((\bar{b}+\bar{c})+(b\mspace{};\mspace{.5}c)\mspace{};\mspace{.5}p\mspace{};\mspace{.5}\operatorname{\mathsf{while}}\mathbin{}(b\land c)\operatorname{\mathsf{do}}\mathbin{}p)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\bar{b}.

To show (

), note that

,

,

, which entail (

.
Equation (

Let us proceed with the proof of (

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathsf{while}}\mathbin{}b\operatorname{\mathsf{do}}\mathbin{}

\displaystyle(\operatorname{\mathsf{if}}\mathbin{}c\operatorname{\mathsf{then}}\mathbin{}p\operatorname{\mathsf{else}}\mathbin{}q)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;(\operatorname{\mathsf{while}}\mathbin{}b\operatorname{\mathsf{do}}\mathbin{}(\operatorname{\mathsf{if}}\mathbin{}c\operatorname{\mathsf{then}}\mathbin{}p\operatorname{\mathsf{else}}\mathbin{}q))\mspace{};\mspace{.5}\bar{b}

/​​/ (
21

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;(\operatorname{\mathsf{while}}\mathbin{}(b\land c)\lor(b\land\bar{c})\operatorname{\mathsf{do}}\mathbin{}(\operatorname{\mathsf{if}}\mathbin{}c\operatorname{\mathsf{then}}\mathbin{}p\operatorname{\mathsf{else}}\mathbin{}q))\mspace{};\mspace{.5}\bar{b}

\displaystyle\;(\operatorname{\mathsf{while}}\mathbin{}(b\land c)\operatorname{\mathsf{do}}\mathbin{}(\operatorname{\mathsf{if}}\mathbin{}c\operatorname{\mathsf{then}}\mathbin{}p\operatorname{\mathsf{else}}\mathbin{}q))\mspace{};\mspace{.5}

\displaystyle\;(\operatorname{\mathsf{while}}\mathbin{}(b\land\bar{c})\operatorname{\mathsf{do}}\mathbin{}((\operatorname{\mathsf{if}}\mathbin{}c\operatorname{\mathsf{then}}\mathbin{}p\operatorname{\mathsf{else}}\mathbin{}q)\mspace{};\mspace{.5}

\displaystyle\hskip 73.50017pt\operatorname{\mathsf{while}}\mathbin{}(b\land c)\operatorname{\mathsf{do}}\mathbin{}(\operatorname{\mathsf{if}}\mathbin{}c\operatorname{\mathsf{then}}\mathbin{}p\operatorname{\mathsf{else}}\mathbin{}q)))\mspace{};\mspace{.5}\bar{b}

/​​/

\displaystyle\;(\operatorname{\mathsf{while}}\mathbin{}(b\land c)\operatorname{\mathsf{do}}\mathbin{}p)\mspace{};\mspace{.5}

/​​/ (

)

Next, observe that

, and hence, by

\displaystyle(\bar{b}\lor\bar{c})\mspace{};\mspace{.5}\operatorname{\mathsf{while}}\mathbin{}

\displaystyle b\operatorname{\mathsf{do}}\mathbin{}(q\mspace{};\mspace{.5}\operatorname{\mathsf{while}}\mathbin{}(b\land c)\operatorname{\mathsf{do}}\mathbin{}p)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\operatorname{\mathsf{while}}\mathbin{}(b\land\bar{c})\operatorname{\mathsf{do}}\mathbin{}(q\mspace{};\mspace{.5}\operatorname{\mathsf{while}}\mathbin{}(b\land c)\operatorname{\mathsf{do}}\mathbin{}p)\mspace{};\mspace{.5}\bar{b}.

Hence, we can reduce the right-hand side of (

) to the same expression:

\displaystyle(\operatorname{\mathsf{while}}\mathbin{}(b\,

\displaystyle\land c)\operatorname{\mathsf{do}}\mathbin{}p)\mspace{};\mspace{.5}\operatorname{\mathsf{while}}\mathbin{}b\operatorname{\mathsf{do}}\mathbin{}(q\mspace{};\mspace{.5}\operatorname{\mathsf{while}}\mathbin{}(b\land c)\operatorname{\mathsf{do}}\mathbin{}p)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;(\operatorname{\mathsf{while}}\mathbin{}(b\land c)\operatorname{\mathsf{do}}\mathbin{}p)\mspace{};\mspace{.5}(\bar{b}\lor\bar{c})\mspace{};\mspace{.5}\operatorname{\mathsf{while}}\mathbin{}b\operatorname{\mathsf{do}}\mathbin{}(q\mspace{};\mspace{.5}\operatorname{\mathsf{while}}\mathbin{}(b\land c)\operatorname{\mathsf{do}}\mathbin{}p)

/​​/ (
21
)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;(\operatorname{\mathsf{while}}\mathbin{}(b\land c)\operatorname{\mathsf{do}}\mathbin{}p)\mspace{};\mspace{.5}\operatorname{\mathsf{while}}\mathbin{}(b\land\bar{c})\operatorname{\mathsf{do}}\mathbin{}(q\mspace{};\mspace{.5}\operatorname{\mathsf{while}}\mathbin{}(b\land c)\operatorname{\mathsf{do}}\mathbin{}p)\mspace{};\mspace{.5}\bar{b}.

IX.
Proof of
Theorem 23

First, observe that
\mathbf{V}_{{\bm{\mathsf{T}}}_{H}}
is an idempotent grove category under the following
semilattice structure on
\mathbf{V}(X,T_{H}Y)
: the bottom element is
0\mspace{};\mspace{.5}\mathsf{out}^{\text{\kern 0.5pt-}\mathsf{\kern-0.5pt1}}
,
and the join of
f,g\colon{X\to T_{H}Y}
is
(f\mspace{};\mspace{.5}\mathsf{out}+g\mspace{};\mspace{.5}\mathsf{out})\mspace{};\mspace{.5}\mathsf{out}^{\text{\kern 0.5pt-}\mathsf{\kern-0.5pt1}}
.

For every
f\in\mathbf{V}(X,TY)
,
f\mspace{};\mspace{.5}T\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{0}}}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}\mathsf{out}^{\text{\kern 0.5pt-}\mathsf{\kern-0.5pt1}}\in\mathbf{V}(X,T_{H}Y)
, which yields
a functor
J\colon\mathbf{V}_{{\bm{\mathsf{T}}}}\to\mathbf{V}_{{\bm{\mathsf{T}}}_{H}}
. The action of
J
on morphisms is monomorphic
– moreover, it is a section: for every
g\in\mathbf{V}(X,T_{H}Y)
, let
Rg=g\mspace{};\mspace{.5}\mathsf{out}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}[\eta,0]^{\sharp}\in\mathbf{V}(X,TY)
; then

\displaystyle R(Jf)=

\displaystyle\;f\mspace{};\mspace{.5}T\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{0}}}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}\mathsf{out}^{\text{\kern 0.5pt-}\mathsf{\kern-0.5pt1}}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}\mathsf{out}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}[\eta,0]^{\sharp}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;f\mspace{};\mspace{.5}T\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{0}}}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}[\eta,0]^{\sharp}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;f\mspace{};\mspace{.5}(\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{0}}}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}[\eta,0])^{\sharp}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;f.

Thus,
\mathbf{V}_{{\bm{\mathsf{T}}}}
is a wide subcategory of
\mathbf{V}_{{\bm{\mathsf{T}}}_{H}}
. Since
\accentset{\rule{4.50697pt}{0.6pt}}{\mathbf{C}}
is a
wide subcategory of
\mathbf{V}_{{\bm{\mathsf{T}}}}
, by transitivity,
\accentset{\rule{4.50697pt}{0.6pt}}{\mathbf{C}}
is a
wide subcategory of
\mathbf{V}_{{\bm{\mathsf{T}}}_{H}}
. We proceed to prove that
{(\accentset{\rule{4.50697pt}{0.6pt}}{\mathbf{C}},\mathbf{V}_{{\bm{\mathsf{T}}}_{H}})}

is a KiC. By
Theorem 29
, we equivalently prove that

\mathbf{V}_{{\bm{\mathsf{T}}}_{H}}
supports
\accentset{\rule{4.50697pt}{0.6pt}}{\mathbf{C}}
-uniform Conway iteration.

It is already known
[
19
, Lemma 7.2]
that if
{\bm{\mathsf{T}}}
supports Conway iteration
then so does
{\bm{\mathsf{T}}}_{H}
.
By
Theorem 29
, we
are left to check that
{\bm{\mathsf{T}}}_{H}
satisfies
Uniformity
. To that end, we recall how
Elgot iteration
(\operatorname{-\!-})^{\ddagger}
for
{\bm{\mathsf{T}}}_{H}
is defined via Elgot
iteration
(\operatorname{-\!-})^{\ddagger}
for
{\bm{\mathsf{T}}}
. Given
f\colon X\to T_{H}(Y\oplus X)
,

f^{\ddagger}\colon X\to T_{H}Y
is the unique solution of the equation

\displaystyle f^{\ddagger}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}\mathsf{out}=

X.
Proof of
35

The dependency condition is obvious in all three cases. We will prove finiteness
of sets of derivatives only.

(Sum)
Let
t,s\in T_{\nu}n
be rational. Then
\mathfrak{D}(t+s)=\{t+s\}\cup\mathfrak{D}(t)\cup\mathfrak{D}(s)
,
which is finite, since
\mathfrak{D}(t)
and
\mathfrak{D}(s)
are so.

(Composition)
It suffices to stick to the following instance: given

n,k\in{\rm Nature}
, a rational element
t\in T_{\nu}n
and a rational map
s\colon n\to T_{\nu}k
, show that
t\mspace{};\mspace{.5}s^{\sharp}\in T_{\nu}k

is rational.
Consider the set
P
of sums of the form

\displaystyle t^{\prime}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}s^{\sharp}+\sum_{s^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{D}(s)}r_{s^{\prime}}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}(s^{\prime})^{\sharp}

where
t^{\prime}
ranges over
\mathfrak{D}(t)
and
r_{s^{\prime}}
range over
non-guarded elements of
T_{\nu}n
. Then
P
is finite.
Moreover,
P
is closed under derivatives: using
32
,

\displaystyle\partial_{b,\mathsf{u}}(t^{\prime}

\displaystyle\mspace{};\mspace{.5}s^{\sharp}+\sum_{s^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{D}(s)}r_{s^{\prime}}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}(s^{\prime})^{\sharp})

\displaystyle\;=\partial_{b,\mathsf{u}}(t^{\prime})\mspace{};\mspace{.5}s^{\sharp}+o(t^{\prime})\mspace{};\mspace{.5}(\partial_{b,\mathsf{u}}(s))^{\sharp}+\sum_{s^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{D}(s)}\partial_{b,\mathsf{u}}(r_{s^{\prime}})\mspace{};\mspace{.5}(s^{\prime})^{\sharp}

\displaystyle\qquad+\sum_{s^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{D}(s)}o(r_{s^{\prime}})\mspace{};\mspace{.5}(\partial_{b,\mathsf{u}}(s^{\prime}))^{\sharp}

\displaystyle\;=\partial_{b,\mathsf{u}}(t^{\prime})\mspace{};\mspace{.5}s^{\sharp}+o(t^{\prime})\mspace{};\mspace{.5}(\partial_{b,\mathsf{u}}(s))^{\sharp}+\sum_{s^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{D}(s)}o(r_{s^{\prime}})\mspace{};\mspace{.5}(\partial_{b,\mathsf{u}}(s^{\prime}))^{\sharp}

\displaystyle\;=\partial_{b,\mathsf{u}}(t^{\prime})\mspace{};\mspace{.5}s^{\sharp}+(o(t^{\prime})+o(r_{s}))\mspace{};\mspace{.5}(\partial_{b,\mathsf{u}}(s))^{\sharp}

\displaystyle\qquad+\sum_{s^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{D}(s)\smallsetminus\{s\}}o(r_{s^{\prime}})\mspace{};\mspace{.5}(\partial_{b,\mathsf{u}}(s^{\prime}))^{\sharp},

and analogously for
\partial_{b,\mathsf{f}}^{k}
. Note that
t\mspace{};\mspace{.5}s^{\sharp}\in P
. Therefore

\mathfrak{D}(t\mspace{};\mspace{.5}s^{\sharp})\subseteq P
. Since
P
is finite, so is
\mathfrak{D}(t\mspace{};\mspace{.5}s^{\sharp})
.

(Iteration)
Let
t=[t_{0},\ldots,t_{n-1}]\colon n\to T_{\nu}n
, and
\mathfrak{D}(t_{i})

be finite for
i=0,\ldots,n-1
. Analogously to the previous clause, consider the set

P
of sums of the form

\displaystyle\sum_{t^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{D}(t)}r_{t^{\prime}}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}(t^{\prime})^{\sharp}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}(t^{\ast})^{\sharp}+r

where
t^{\prime}
ranges over
\mathfrak{D}(t)
and
r_{s^{\prime}}
,
r
range over those
non-guarded elements of
T_{\nu}n
. In the same manner as in
the previous clause:
P
is finite, contains
t^{\ast}
and is closed under derivatives,
hence
\mathfrak{D}(t^{\ast})
is finite.∎

XII.
Proof of
Theorem 37

We rely on the following observation.

Lemma 41
.

Let
(\mathbf{C},\accentset{\rule{4.50697pt}{0.6pt}}{\mathbf{C}})
and
(\mathbf{D},\accentset{\rule{4.50697pt}{0.6pt}}{\mathbf{D}})
be two KiCs, and let
F
be the following
map, acting on objects and on morphisms:
FX\in|\mathbf{D}|
for every
X\in|\mathbf{C}|
,

Fp\in\mathbf{D}(FX,FY)
for every
p\in\mathbf{C}(X,Y)
. Suppose that
F
preserves coproducts,
Fp\in\accentset{\rule{4.50697pt}{0.6pt}}{\mathbf{D}}(FX,FY)
for all
p\in\accentset{\rule{4.50697pt}{0.6pt}}{\mathbf{C}}(X,Y)
,
and the following further preservation properties hold

\displaystyle Fp^{\ast}=(Fp)^{\ast},\quad F(p\mspace{};\mspace{.5}\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{0}}})=Fp\mspace{};\mspace{.5}\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{0}}},\quad F(p\mspace{};\mspace{.5}\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{1}}})=Fp\mspace{};\mspace{.5}\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{1}}},\quad F(p\mspace{};\mspace{.5}[0,1])=Fp\mspace{};\mspace{.5}[0,1].

Then
F
is a KiC-functor.

Proof.

Note that the assumptions entail that
1
and
0
are also preserved
by
F
:
F1=F(\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{0}}}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}[1,0])=\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{0}}}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}[1,0]=1
,
F0=F(\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{1}}}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}[1,0])=\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{1}}}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}[1,0]=0
.
We need to show that the assumptions entail that
F
preserves nondeterministic sums and morphism
compositions.

(Compositions)
Let
p\in\mathbf{C}(X,Y)
,
q\in\mathbf{C}(Y,Z)
, and show that
F(p\mspace{};\mspace{.5}q)=Fp\mspace{};\mspace{.5}Fq
.
Note that, essentially by
\ast
-Fix
,

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{00}}}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}[[p\mspace{};\mspace{.5}

\displaystyle\,\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{10}}},q\mspace{};\mspace{.5}\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{1}}}],0]^{\ast}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}[0,1]

\displaystyle=\;

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{00}}}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}[0,1]+\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{00}}}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}[[p\mspace{};\mspace{.5}\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{10}}},q\mspace{};\mspace{.5}\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{1}}}],0]\mspace{};\mspace{.5}[[p\mspace{};\mspace{.5}\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{10}}},q\mspace{};\mspace{.5}\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{1}}}],0]^{\ast}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}[0,1]

\displaystyle=\;

\displaystyle p\mspace{};\mspace{.5}\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{10}}}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}[[p\mspace{};\mspace{.5}\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{10}}},q\mspace{};\mspace{.5}\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{1}}}],0]^{\ast}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}[0,1]

\displaystyle=\;

\displaystyle p\mspace{};\mspace{.5}(\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{10}}}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}[0,1]+\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{10}}}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}[[p\mspace{};\mspace{.5}\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{10}}},q\mspace{};\mspace{.5}\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{1}}}],0]\mspace{};\mspace{.5}[[p\mspace{};\mspace{.5}\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{10}}},q\mspace{};\mspace{.5}\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{1}}}],0]^{\ast}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}[0,1])

\displaystyle=\;

\displaystyle p\mspace{};\mspace{.5}q\mspace{};\mspace{.5}\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{1}}}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}[[p\mspace{};\mspace{.5}\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{10}}},q\mspace{};\mspace{.5}\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{1}}}],0]^{\ast}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}[0,1]

\displaystyle=\;

\displaystyle p\mspace{};\mspace{.5}q\mspace{};\mspace{.5}(\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{1}}}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}[0,1]+\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{1}}}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}[[p\mspace{};\mspace{.5}\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{10}}},q\mspace{};\mspace{.5}\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{1}}}],0]\mspace{};\mspace{.5}[[p\mspace{};\mspace{.5}\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{10}}},q\mspace{};\mspace{.5}\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{1}}}],0]^{\ast}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}[0,1])

\displaystyle=\;

\displaystyle p\mspace{};\mspace{.5}q,

and hence

\displaystyle F(p\mspace{};\mspace{.5}q)=\;

\displaystyle F(\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{00}}}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}[[p\mspace{};\mspace{.5}\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{10}}},q\mspace{};\mspace{.5}\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{1}}}],0]^{\ast}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}[0,1])

\displaystyle=\;

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{00}}}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}[[Fp\mspace{};\mspace{.5}\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{10}}},Fq\mspace{};\mspace{.5}\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{1}}}],0]^{\ast}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}[0,1])

\displaystyle=\;

\displaystyle Fp\mspace{};\mspace{.5}Fq.

(Sums)
Note that, essentially by
\ast
-Fix
,
\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{0}}}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}[\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{1}}},0]^{\ast}=\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{0}}}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}(1+[\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{1}}},0]\mspace{};\mspace{.5}[\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{1}}},0]^{\ast})=\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{0}}}+\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{1}}}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}[\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{1}}},0]^{\ast}=\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{0}}}+\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{1}}}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}(1+[\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{1}}},0]\mspace{};\mspace{.5}[\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{1}}},0]^{\ast})=\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{0}}}+\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{1}}}
. Thus,
for any
p,q\in\mathbf{C}(X,Y)
, using the previous clause,

\displaystyle F(p+q)=\;

\displaystyle F((\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{0}}}+\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{1}}})\mspace{};\mspace{.5}[p,q])

\displaystyle=\;

\displaystyle F(\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{0}}}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}[\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{1}}},0]^{\ast}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}[p,q])

\displaystyle=\;

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{0}}}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}[\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{1}}},0]^{\ast}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}[Fp,Fq]

\displaystyle=\;

\displaystyle(\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{0}}}+\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{1}}})\mspace{};\mspace{.5}[Fp,Fq]

\displaystyle=\;

\displaystyle Fp+Fq,

and we are done.
∎

The defined lifting
\llbracket\operatorname{-\!-}\rrbracket_{\shortuparrow}\colon\mathfrak{F}\to\mathbf{C}
is easily seen to
make (
9
) commute. Also, note that there is no more than one structure-preserving
candidate for
\llbracket\operatorname{-\!-}\rrbracket_{\shortuparrow}
, to make (
9
) commute: indeed,
since every morphism in
\mathfrak{F}
is representable as
t=\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{n,m}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}s^{\ast}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}r^{\sharp}
,

\llbracket t\rrbracket_{\shortuparrow}
must only be defined as
\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{n,m}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}\llbracket s\rrbracket^{\ast}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}\llbracket r\rrbracket
.

We are left to check that
\llbracket\operatorname{-\!-}\rrbracket_{\shortuparrow}
is a KiCT-functor, which is
facilitated by
41
. The only non-trivial clause is
preservation of Kleene star. As an auxiliary step, we show that

\displaystyle\llbracket\eta+\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{n,m}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}s^{\ast}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}r^{\sharp}\rrbracket_{\shortuparrow}=\llbracket\eta\rrbracket_{\shortuparrow}+\llbracket\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{n,m}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}s^{\ast}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}r^{\sharp}\rrbracket_{\shortuparrow}

(106)

for any guarded flat
s\colon m\to T_{\nu}m
, and a non-guarded
r\colon m\to T_{\nu}n
.
In order to calculate the left-hand side of (
106
), we need to
find a suitable representation for
1+\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{n,m}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}s^{\ast}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}r^{\sharp}
.
Concretely, we show that

\displaystyle\eta+\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{n,m}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}s^{\ast}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}r^{\sharp}=\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{n,m+m}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}[\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{1}}}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}\eta,s\mspace{};\mspace{.5}T_{\nu}\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{1}}}]^{\ast}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}[[\eta_{n},0],r]^{\sharp}

Indeed, using
\ast
-Fix
and
\ast
-Uni
,

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{n,m+m}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}

\displaystyle[\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{1}}}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}\eta,s\mspace{};\mspace{.5}T_{\nu}\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{1}}}]^{\ast}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}[[\eta_{n},0],r]^{\sharp}

\displaystyle=\;

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{n,m+m}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}(\eta+[\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{1}}}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}\eta,s\mspace{};\mspace{.5}T_{\nu}\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{1}}}]\mspace{};\mspace{.5}([\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{1}}}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}\eta,s\mspace{};\mspace{.5}T_{\nu}\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{1}}}]^{\ast})^{\sharp})\mspace{};\mspace{.5}[[\eta_{n},0],r]^{\sharp}

\displaystyle=\;

\displaystyle\eta+\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{n,m}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{1}}}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}\eta\mspace{};\mspace{.5}([\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{1}}}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}\eta,s\mspace{};\mspace{.5}T_{\nu}\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{1}}}]^{\ast})^{\sharp}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}[[\eta_{n},0],r]^{\sharp}

\displaystyle=\;

\displaystyle\eta+\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{n,m}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}s^{\ast}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}T_{\nu}\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{1}}}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}[[\eta_{n},0],r]^{\sharp}

\displaystyle=\;

\displaystyle\eta+\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{n,m}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}s^{\ast}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}r^{\sharp}.

Now, (
106
) turns into

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{n,m+m}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}[\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{1}}},\llbracket s\rrbracket\mspace{};\mspace{.5}\operatorname{\mathsf{in_{1}}}]^{\ast}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}[[1_{n},0],\llbracket r\rrbracket]=1+\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{n,m}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}\llbracket s\rrbracket^{\ast}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}\llbracket r\rrbracket.

This equation is shown as above, since
\ast
-Fix
and
\ast
-Uni

are sound for
\mathbf{C}
.

An analogous method is used to show that
\llbracket\operatorname{-\!-}\rrbracket_{\shortuparrow}
preserves Kleene star.
Let
s\colon m\to T_{\nu}m
be guarded flat, and let
r\colon m\to T_{\nu}n
be non-guarded,
and prove that:

\displaystyle\llbracket(\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{n,m}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}s^{\ast}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}r^{\sharp})^{\ast}\rrbracket_{\shortuparrow}=\llbracket\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{n,m}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}s^{\ast}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}r^{\sharp}\rrbracket_{\shortuparrow}^{\ast}.

(107)

The following equation is provable using the axioms of KiC

\displaystyle(\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{n,m}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}s^{\ast}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}r^{\sharp})^{\ast}=\eta+\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{n,m}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}((r\mspace{};\mspace{.5}T_{\nu}\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{n,m})^{\ast}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}s^{\sharp})^{\ast}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}((r\mspace{};\mspace{.5}T_{\nu}\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{n,m})^{\ast}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}r^{\sharp})^{\sharp},

hence, the equation

\displaystyle(\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{n,m}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}\llbracket s\rrbracket^{\ast}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}\llbracket r\rrbracket^{\sharp})^{\ast}=1+\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{n,m}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}((\llbracket r\rrbracket\mspace{};\mspace{.5}\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{n,m})^{\ast}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}\llbracket s\rrbracket)^{\ast}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}(\llbracket r\rrbracket\mspace{};\mspace{.5}\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{n,m})^{\ast}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}\llbracket r\rrbracket

is provable as well. Now, the proof of (
107
) is as follows:

\displaystyle\!\!\!\!\llbracket(\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{n,m}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}s^{\ast}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}r^{\sharp})^{\ast}\rrbracket_{\shortuparrow}=\;

\displaystyle\llbracket\eta+\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{n,m}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}((r\mspace{};\mspace{.5}T_{\nu}\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{n,m})^{\ast}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}s^{\sharp})^{\ast}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}((r\mspace{};\mspace{.5}T_{\nu}\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{n,m})^{\ast}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}r^{\sharp})^{\sharp}\rrbracket_{\shortuparrow}

\displaystyle=\;

\displaystyle 1+\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{n,m}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}((\llbracket r\rrbracket\mspace{};\mspace{.5}\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{n,m})^{\ast}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}\llbracket s\rrbracket^{\sharp})^{\ast}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}(\llbracket r\rrbracket\mspace{};\mspace{.5}\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{n,m})^{\ast}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}\llbracket r\rrbracket

/​​/ (
106
)

\displaystyle=\;

\displaystyle(\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{n,m}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}\llbracket s\rrbracket^{\ast}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}\llbracket r\rrbracket^{\sharp})^{\ast}

\displaystyle=\;

\displaystyle\llbracket\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{n,m}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}s^{\ast}\mspace{};\mspace{.5}r^{\sharp}\rrbracket_{\shortuparrow}^{\ast}.
